‘annihilating vehemence’ (p. 71) of the animal. Carter’s ‘Beauty’ 
offers herself as sacrifice to this Beast, as in the original story, but 
understands ‘his appetite need not be my extinction’ (p. 74). She 
becomes an animal, after also rejecting her father’s world, in a union 
of sensual pleasure with The Beast. The tiger is a symbol of realised 
mutual passion that affords men and women equal respect, and 
regards sexual desire as beautiful and natural. In becoming most 
true to the animal within the human, men and women can become 
‘fully human’ (‘The Lady of the House of Love’, p. 124).

Betty Friedan's The

Themes 
Marriage 
Marriage is identified in the tales with the past, with corruption and 
with deception. Though the first story in the collection opens with 
the new bride eagerly looking forward to and travelling excitedly 
towards her future life in marriage with the Marquis, ‘The Bloody 
Chamber’ portrays marriage as a moral and literal equivalent of 
death. Similarly, in ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’, the marriage 
contract is an economic exchange of a commodity where a woman, 
owned and controlled by the father, passes into the ownership of 
another man. This is a historically accurate representation of 
social conventions in marriage which were disguised, but not 
fundamentally changed, by later romantic views of marriage. 
Men no longer own and control women in marriage, because the 
old laws that defined all joint property in marriage as belonging 
to the husband have now changed. But Carter is suggesting that 
men behave as if they still have the right to control women, and 
her stories challenge that expectation of male control. 
The passing of the female from the care of the father to the care of 
the husband, symbolically enacted in the civil and religious rituals 
of the wedding ceremony, is mocked in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’. The 
‘parlour’ (p. 57) where the father gambles away his daughter to 
‘La Bestia’ (p. 57) is a substitute casino that emphasises the role 
that chance plays in human relationships. Yet she, as ‘a woman of 
honour’ (p. 68), is bound by the contract or bargain struck over 
‘A queen, a king, an ace’ (p. 59). Again, Carter is pointing out 
8o The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===
Critical approaches Themes 
the economic circumstances or financial arrangements that 
govern the actions of individuals as much as their personalities 
or beliefs. 
In ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ the bankrupted father brings 
‘Miss Lamb’ (p. 48) to lie with ‘Mr Lyon’ (p. 55) after a dispute 
about the limits of hospitality. Her cooperation or compliance with 
the arrangements made between the men guarantees, ‘on some 
magically reciprocal scale, the price of her father’s good fortune’ 
(p. 48). The benefits of economic alliances formed through marriage 
arrangements, similar across many cultures despite taking different 
forms, are demonstrated in her ‘new-found prosperity’ (p. 51). Yet 
she is not committed to a marriage at this point in the story. Beauty 
and her father enjoy ‘life ... as she had never known it’ on ‘credit’, 
so their status, ‘as good as rich’ (p. 51), is a temporary matter and 
subject to terms and conditions. Her absence from the Beast’s home 
causes his domestic affairs to fall into a state of advanced neglect and 
‘disillusion’ (p. 53). Flower arranging is an activity stereotypically 
associated with the bourgeois housewife, generally taken for 
granted by their husbands. The dying flowers symbolise not 
only the Beast’s romantic enervation but also hint at the future 
Miss Lamb will have as Mrs Lyon: domestic servant. 
‘Wolf-Alice’ and ‘The Company of Wolves’ portray different 
versions of partnership between the sexes which have little to 
do with any recognisable ceremony or convention of marriage. 
Although Wolf-Alice is dressed as a ‘white bride’, her ‘pitiful’ 
(p. 148) ‘ministrations’ (p. 149) to the wounds of the Duke at the 
transformative end of the story are not conducted through any 
aspect of civilised behaviour. Marriage is a major aspect of human 
civilisation, but Wolf-Alice exists outside all such norms. That is 
why she can lead the Duke into a realisation of his identity beyond 
the traditional values of patriarchy. Carter is suggesting that any 
new realignment of male and female relationships can only be 
‘brought into being by her’ (p. 149), even though ‘she lives without 
a future’ (p. 141). This implies a recognition that the idealisation 
of marriage as a binding contract, or life sentence, is difficult to 
sustain. 
‘The Company of Wolves’ shows through its table-turning 
conclusion that women can have relationships with men on their

in feminine form. In

own terms by taking charge of their own destiny. Carter’s ‘savage 
marriage ceremony’ (p. 139) is a prelude to a montage of images that 
conveys the idea of future peace and prosperity, the ‘happily ever 
after’ promised in every fairy tale yet rarely glimpsed in reality. 
The ‘blizzard died down’ after the violence of the tale to leave 
the forest ‘All silent, all still’ on ‘Christmas Day’ (p. 139), Carter’s 
words echoing the refrain of the well-known Christian carol ‘Silent 
Night’, concluding her tale with a hopeful and powerful metaphor 
of reconciliation. 
Sexuality 
The tales as a whole offer an impression of sexuality as an equally 
frightening and exciting aspect of adult behaviour; Carter does not 
shrink from portraying the extremes of desire in detailing human 
behaviour at its most perverse. In ‘The Bloody Chamber’ the risks 
involved in allowing another person to define one’s sexuality, in 
being submissive and passive in a relationship, are given an urgent 
imperative nature. Carter does flirt with sadomasochistic 
psychology here, but ultimately rejects masochistic submission 
as empty and unfulfilling. 
In ‘The Bloody Chamber’ Carter emphasises the appeal of the risk 
in marrying an older, more experienced man. The attention and 
prestige the pianist acquires through her association with a powerful 
man are flattering to her. She, who knows ‘nothing of the world’ 
(p. 4), finds herself caught in conflicting emotional responses that 
give rise to ‘a kind of fear’ at the ‘strange, impersonal arousal’ and 
‘repugnance’ (p. 11) she feels when her husband delays the moment 
of consummation of their marriage. But it is more a fear of her 
sexual response that is being described. The anticipation of the event 
is far more pleasurable than the event itself, the Marquis suggests, 
and that is certainly true for her. 
The pianist’s disturbing realisation that she, like the Marquis, is 
capable of almost anything is a moment of self-awareness: ‘I sensed 
in myself a potentiality for corruption that took my breath away’ 
(p. 6). But it is also a realisation of how very far from corrupt 
she is, having led an ‘innocent and confined life’ (p. 6). As she is 
stripped in the ‘formal disrobing of the bride’ (p. 11), her responses 
are ambiguous. The ‘trembling’ (p. 10), blushing and changed 
82 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 85 ===
1 
Critical approaches Themes 
breathing pattern could be understood equally to be nervousness 
or sexual arousal, until she confesses: ‘I was aghast to feel myself 
stirring’ (p. 11). But the complexity of this physical sensation is 
based on the fact that she has ‘seen [her] flesh in his eyes’ (p. 11); 
her desire is aroused by his voyeurism, suggesting exhibitionism 
on her part. The Marquis’s pleasures are visual; he enjoys looking at 
her. Her surprise is that she seems to enjoy being looked at by him 
in this way. This was a controversial sentiment for a feminist writer 
to give to a female character in the 1970s, particularly as it comes 
close to confirming a male chauvinist attitude around at the time 
that women enjoyed being dominated and humiliated. 
The narrator is entirely passive at this stage and does not anticipate 
anything but being a recipient of male attention: ‘my husband beds 
me’ (p. 12). After losing her virginity, she recalls the moment with 
hints of the violence involved. The Marquis seems so exhausted, he 
might have been ‘fighting’ with her; his composure was shattered 
‘like a porcelain vase flung against a wall’; she heard him ‘shriek 
and blaspheme’ (p. 14); and there was blood. Carter has assembled 
in the new wife’s recount of the moment of ‘the orgasm’ a careful 
construct of images of domestic violence. Her ‘spent body’ (p. 14) 
has been used up; her virginity consumed by the Marquis as 
‘connoisseur’ (p. 13) and ‘gourmand’ (p. 11). Her unfulfilled desire, 
which seems to survive the onslaught of the Marquis’s attentions, 
is thwarted by his decision to abandon her on the honeymoon for 
business. Her frustration is expressed when she has ‘to be content’ 
(p. 15) with only having dinner with him. 
Human sexuality is clearly conceived by Carter as something 
that is dangerous and animalistic - as demonstrated by the 
violent images in ‘The Bloody Chamber’; the voyeurism and 
exhibitionism in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’; the comical contrast of the 
mundane rutting cats and the exuberant coupling of the lovers 
in ‘Puss-in-Boots’; the imprisonment of the women in ‘The 
Erl-King’; the intimate moments of transformation in ‘The Tiger’s 
Bride’, ‘The Company of Wolves’ and ‘Wolf-Alice’ - but desire 
always remains contained within the boundaries of heterosexual 
behaviour. The various endings of the tales offer different insights 
into the possibilities of resolving the contradictions Carter 
perceives in male and female relationships.

QUESTION 
When Carter 
explores the 
dynamics of persona! 
terror in 'The Bloody 
Chamber', do you 
think her writing 
is literary 
entertainment or 
feminist 
propaganda? 
The view on sexuality within the stories is exclusively a female view, 
presented as a challenge to the prevailing attitudes of the 1970s. 
Male sexuality is represented mostly as aggressive and selfish. The 
men in the stories are not presented with any life-threatening rite of 
passage or dilemma in pursuing their sexual destinies. The Marquis 
is a serial killer who has followed in the footsteps of his ancestors to 
enter ‘the kingdom of the unimaginable’ (‘The Bloody Chamber’, 
p. 36). Mr Lyon, lying down with Miss Lamb at the end of ‘The 
Courtship of Mr Lyon’, can depend on her self-sacrifice to sustain 
his existence. The advances of The Beast in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ are 
only rejected, at first, because they are so timid. Puss-in-Boots is 
an insensitive braggart: ‘what lady in all the world could say “no” 
to ... a fine marmalade cat?’ (‘Puss-in-Boots’, p. 76). His master has 
practical problems to overcome to gain access to the lady who is the 
object of his desire, but these matters are resolved for him by the 
female tabby cat. The soldier in ‘The Lady of the House of Love’ 
will not take ‘criminal advantage’ (p. 122) of the vampire he believes 
to be in need of medical treatment for ‘nervous hysteria’ (p. 124). 
The certainty of all these men, their self-assurance, is their most 
deadly attribute. 
The roots of Carter’s portrayal of women’s fears of sex lie in an 
awareness of the long history for women of sexual activity being a 
calculated risk with possibly fatal consequences. The real, physical 
consequences of desire for women are unavoidably linked with pain. 
Sexuality is seen as a kind of portal through which a woman passes 
towards possible fulfilment, perhaps motherhood, or even death. 
The sensation of fear, particularly of fear that is proved or known to 
be unfounded or unreal, is stimulating. It is this thrill that underpins 
the psychology of the reading experience in the Gothic and horror 
genres; humans find fear a pleasurable sensation, up to a point. 
The bride does not mention any personal fear of the Marquis 
until she is approaching the forbidden chamber itself, and only 
acknowledges a ‘dreadful anguish’ (p. 28) when she realises she is to 
join this macabre display of dead wives. When she mistakes the sound 
of Jean-Yves for her husband returning she finds that ‘fear gave me 
strength’ (p. 30). Yet the return of her husband does cause genuine 
fear and she only has sufficient resourcefulness to delay the moment 
of execution long enough for her mother to secure her rescue. 
84 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 87 ===
Critical approaches Themes 
This thrill of risk-taking accompanies all the experiences of the 
young women in these tales as they find themselves confronted by 
strange men who exert power over them of one kind or another. 
Even ‘Beauty’ in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’, beneath her moral indignation, 
is a little disappointed to discover the Beast has not asked for more 
than a quick look at her naked: ‘That he should want so little was 
the reason why I could not give it’ (p. 68). 
Metamorphosis 
Changing the forms of things to reveal some idea of truth is a 
common theme of folk and fairy tales. In the original ‘Beauty and 
the Beast’ the creature is rescued from brutality by the goodness of 
a true woman, who restores him to virtue and his handsome self. 
Carter’s two takes on this offer something quite different. Mr Lyon 
loses his attractive and powerful animal qualities and ends up 
looking ‘unkempt’ (‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’, p. 54), having 
acquired ‘a broken nose, such as the noses of retired boxers’ 
(pp. 54-5). The impression that he is, in fact, not such a handsome 
beast after all is hard to avoid. ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ dispenses with 
such tongue-in-cheek tactics and passes the transformation to 
‘Beauty’, who - having dispatched her replica to the real world of 
her avaricious father - is free to shed ‘all the skins of a life in the 
world’ (p. 75) and become a beast herself. Carter inverts the 
symbolic form as she inverts the tale. To be beast-like is to be 
virtuous; to become ‘manly’ is to be vicious. 
‘The Snow Child’ offers a different view on what is real in desire 
and how what is desired cannot always be realised. The brevity 
of this tale does not exaggerate the changes that take place here 
in swift succession, but it is worth considering how the Count’s 
wishes become manifest in the world, and how the Countess’s 
wishes change her situation. The transitory nature of male desire 
is emphasised by the vanishing and melting away of the child: 
‘Soon there was nothing left of her but a feather ... a bloodstain ... 
and the rose’ (p. 106). 
‘The Lady of the House of Love’, with its beautiful Romanian 
aristocratic vampire being transformed into a ‘far older, less 
beautiful’ woman (p. 124), turns ‘Sleeping Beauty’ upside down. 
In the original tale, the princess pricks her finger on a poisoned

In his introduction

object and is about to die. Saved by the limited magical powers 
of good fairies, the princess does not die but lies sleeping until 
magically restored by the kiss of a handsome prince who has 
fought his way through the undergrowth to rescue her. The moral 
conveyed celebrates the virtue of patience, and suggests that the 
endurance of suffering worthy of reward. The vampire cannot sleep 
(here a euphemism for death) and is only able to find some peace 
after cutting her finger, an event shifted to a different point in the 
story. The handsome prince does not kiss her, not in any romantic 
or sexual sense, and is too dim to understand the changes going on 
around him. Carter makes the Countess become ‘less beautiful’ 
(p. 124) after her lover tries to ‘kiss it better’ (p. 123), inverting the 
original tale where Sleeping Beauty is restored to health. Although 
the Countess and her soldier are separated, unlike Prince Charming 
and Sleeping Beauty, it becomes clear the Countess will find some 
kind of union with her lover, and many others, in death. 
The transformations of the wolf, that potent symbol of the 
ferocity of nature, the dangers of the unknown and all-consuming 
desire, are played out over the different versions of the ‘Little Red 
Riding Hood’ tale, though it is a feature of ‘The Company 
of Wolves’ that the metamorphosis is thwarted by the ‘wise 
child’ (p. 138). The threat of the werewolf is tamed by his 
total transformation into a wolf. With his dangerous nature 
domesticated, unable to return to the form of a man, he is now 
merely a ‘tender wolf’ (p. 139). Carter here draws on another 
strand of folk tale which gives women credit for having the wit 
to outsmart the devil himself. 
Beauty and wealth 
A recurring theme in the stories is that beauty can but should not 
be purchased or owned by those who control vast wealth. In ‘The 
Bloody Chamber’ the bride is bought with jewels and clothes; 
elsewhere Beauty is bought through unscrupulous dealing, both 
financial and at the card table: in each case wealth corrupts beauty. 
The ‘tell-tale stain’ on the pianist’s forehead takes ‘the shape and 
brilliance of the heart on a playing card’ (‘The Bloody Chamber’, 
p. 36). Beauty in ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ becomes vain; ‘she 
smiled at herself in mirrors a little too often’ (p. 52). ‘Beauty’ in 
86 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===
Critical approaches Themes 
The Tiger’s Bride’ learns to accept that some truth is contained in 
her father’s cynical axiom ‘that, if you have enough money, 
anything is possible’ (p. 69). In her clockwork servant’s mirror she 
can only see her father’s face reflected as if she ‘had put on his face’ 
(p. 66). It is evident in their appearance and their emotional state 
that being groomed for their master’s pleasure damages them. 
Unlike standard fairy-tale happy endings where the lovers become 
rich, marry and live happily ever after, Carter’s characters show the 
escape from the material world as freedom from corruption. The 
pianist’s redistribution of her dead husband’s wealth is a limited act 
of restorative class justice, with Carter’s revision of the fairy-tale 
formula ‘she became rich and lived happily ever after’ ending in a 
fashion that reflects her socialist principles. 
The Beast in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ wears a mask of a painted ‘beautiful 
face’ but it is ‘too perfect’ to be ‘entirely human’ (p. 58). Carter is 
playing with the reader’s expectations and understanding of the 
character at this point in the story, deliberately keeping the real 
identity of The Beast hidden, but subtle suggestions are made that 
wealth, which enables and requires the adoption of the disguise, 
distances humans from each other and from themselves. Carter is 
influenced here by the Marxist analysis of capitalism and its effects 
on people. Karl Marx (1818-83) described how people become 
obsessed with the things they make, buy, sell and use; unable to 
see themselves in any meaningful way as part of the world. Marx 
described this effect as alienation, where people become strangers 
to themselves. This is clearly represented in the Marquis and 
his private collections of art and literature; and his conspicuous 
consumption of the finest things in life without the necessity of 
working for a living. His personal degradation symbolises the 
degenerate nature of the idle rich and inherited wealth. Wealth is 
not, in itself, corrupting or consoling; it is the product of collective 
human endeavour. In private hands, hoarded for individual 
satisfaction, wealth becomes a destructive power. Carter clearly 
wants us to realise the opposite: that great wealth shared has the 
power to do more good than harm. But part of the problem for 
Carter is that, in presenting the world of luxury with such sensuous 
delight in language - in her rich depiction of grandiose buildings, 
lustrous furnishings, fine clothing and rare works of art - the

Man, woman and nature 
It is easy to forget that the generic use of the masculine noun ‘man’ 
to indicate humanity as a whole, as an abbreviation of ‘mankind’, 
was a common cliche being challenged at the time Carter was 
writing The Bloody Chamber. Carter makes use of ‘types’ 
(see Characterisation) but does not allow one gender ‘type’ to 
misrepresent humanity. Man and nature are depicted as separate 
entities in these tales, while woman is potentially a creature of 
nature. For Carter, womankind is imbued with the power to heal 
the rift between man and nature. 
‘The Erl-King’ shows the spirit of nature as a man, but as a 
peculiarly isolated and feminised man: ‘He is an excellent 
housewife’ (p. 99). In the middle of the wild forest, the Erl-King 
tends to domestic chores, looking after all living and growing things 
in his garden ‘where all the flowers were birds and beasts’ (p. 98). 
Carter describes him as a primitive hunter-gatherer type, with much 
emphasis on his gathering of ‘unnatural treasures’ (p. 99). What 
Carter means by this is not immediately clear, as the next moment 
she narrates a long passage devoted to entirely natural things 
collected by the Erl-King: pigeons’ eggs, dandelions, goat’s milk, 
goat’s cheese and rabbit stew. It is only at the end of that paragraph 
that Carter describes the Erl-King’s ‘cruel’ habit of keeping ‘singing 
birds’ (p. 99) in cages. It is his ‘green eye’ (p. 103) that reduces 
women to the size of birds, and his ‘regard’ (p. 104) that innocently 
imprisons them. Carter’s intertextual references to Shakespeare’s 
play Othello (c. 1602-4), specifically Iago’s ironic warning to 
Othello that jealousy is ‘the green-eyed monster which doth mock / 
The meat it feeds on’ (Act III Scene 3), combine with the notions of 
his ‘regard’ and being ‘looked after’ (p. 103) by him. Carter seems to 
be suggesting that men are ‘naturally’ possessive and jealous, and see 
the world in ways that women do not. She also suggests that women 
are not obliged to be limited by this supposed ‘human nature’ 
of man. 
88 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 91 ===
Critical approaches Themes 
The concept that man hides his true nature behind a mask is 
also presented in ‘The Bloody Chamber’ and ‘The Tiger’s Bride’, 
where the Marquis and The Beast emerge most dangerously when 
the mask is removed. Elsewhere the power of nature is disfigured 
or disguised in man, for example the Beast in ‘The Courtship of 
Mr Lyon’ or the werewolf in the last three stories. It is the figure 
of the werewolf which most clearly presents the relationship 
between man and nature as a perverse and evil trick played upon 
woman: ‘Carnivore incarnate, only immaculate flesh appeases him’ 
(‘The Company of Wolves’, p. 138) is a statement that applies to 
the Marquis in ‘The Bloody Chamber’ as much as it does to the 
wolf in ‘The Company of Wolves’. In repeatedly returning to the 
transformation of this triangular relationship between man, woman 
and nature, Carter challenges the notion that there is such a thing as 
an unchanging human nature or a ‘natural order’ in human society. 
The eponymous character in ‘Wolf-Alice’ clearly expresses the 
problems of over-identification with the inner animal, as it is 
obvious that human society demands a more fastidious attention to 
personal hygiene than nature. Part of being human is being able to 
communicate in sophisticated language, visual and verbal. Language 
is a social phenomenon. Children raised in a wild state of nature do 
not know how to crack the codes of human society. Wolf-Alice 
cannot anticipate or imagine any other moment of time than ‘the 
present tense’ (p. 141). For her there is no past and future, only a 
‘continuous’ (p. 141) moment of the now. While there is ‘sensual 
immediacy’ (p. 141) to her feral existence, there is no future. The 
point here is that we humans can adapt our environment to our 
needs, and have been better and more successful at doing this 
than animals, because of our capacity to imagine alternatives, to 
remember and share the sequences of alternative moments as we 
create the narratives of our lives. Carter is pointing out in her 
stories that our success in adapting the natural environment has 
created a social environment that does not allow all our human 
needs to be adequately fulfilled; and that this is particularly true 
for women because the social environment that has evolved is, she 
would argue, so often a patriarchal one. 
With few exceptions, nature in the stories is a cold and 
unwelcoming place that exists at the point at which human 
civilisation stops. Nature in Carter’s stories can be interpreted

Brothers Grimm

or sign, and also as the inner or instinctive nature that breaks the 
social codes of civilised behaviour. 
The climate is as much a feature of the natural world as the 
landscape in these stories, and most are set in the coldest parts of the 
world in the coldest season. ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ unfolds in 
a hazardous ‘winter’s landscape’ (p. 43) that is slow to leave Mr 
Lyon’s home, though with the image of ‘a drift of fallen petals’ 
(p. 55) spring asserts itself at the conclusion as a more hopeful 
ending. The aristocrats fleeing the Russian winter in ‘The Tiger’s 
Bride’ find no comfort in the ‘treacherous South’ (p. 58) where 
parlours can be ‘cold as hell’ (p. 59). ‘The Erl-King’ takes place in 
an autumnal world with ‘Introspective weather’ (p. 96). ‘The Snow 
Child’ is set in midwinter, as are ‘The Company of Wolves’ and 
‘The Werewolf’. The depressive effect of the cold weather in each 
story in succession is mitigated by settings for other stories which 
are a contrast to that dominant mood. ‘Puss-in-Boots’ reveals itself 
to be anticipating the arrival of the first ‘vernal hint of spring’ (p. 94) 
as the cats and lovers mate so enthusiastically. ‘The Lady of the 
House of Love’ has its main action taking place in ‘hot, ripe 
summer’ (p. Ill) and the events of ‘Wolf-Alice’ span a longer 
period that is not tied to one season. 
The way human behaviour is shaped by a harsh and bleak 
environment is a theme Carter uses frequently. It forms part of the 
introductory preamble to the main story in both ‘The Werewolf’ 
and ‘The Company of Wolves’. The equation of ‘cold weather’ 
and ‘cold hearts’ (p. 126) in the ‘The Werewolf’ is more concretely 
related to a very practical fear of hungry wolves, ‘grey as famine’ 
(‘The Company of Wolves’, p. 130), made more dangerous in 
wintertime when food is scarce. 
The distance from civilisation, or how far society is from being 
truly civilised, is symbolised by the emptiness of the landscape that 
surrounds the fortresses of the patriarchs. The ‘amphibious’ (p. 9) 
isolation of the Marquis’s castle in ‘The Bloody Chamber’ is echoed 
in the ‘bereft landscape’ (p. 70) that is the setting for The Beast’s 
palace in ‘The Tiger’s Bride’, ‘a burned-out planet’ (p. 63) 
dominated by the ‘sad browns and sepias of winter’ (p. 70). 
90 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 93 ===
Critical approaches Themes 
The sea in ‘The Bloody Chamber’ is linked to the matriarch and 
by extension to ideas of the earth mother or ‘Mother Earth’. The 
narrator describes the ‘amniotic salinity of the ocean’ (p. 8) as the 
first sensation recorded on arrival at her destination. This compares 
the sea to the amniotic fluid that surrounds a baby in the womb 
during pregnancy, the most intimate protective layer for the 
developing child. The watery connection between mother and child 
is followed through the ‘melting’ landscape (p. 8), and formed in the 
tears that flow over ‘gold bath taps’ (p. 21) while they talk on the 
telephone. The pianist’s ‘avenging angel’ (p. 39) emerges from the 
sea as the personification of the protective maternal instinct, 
reshaping the pattern of the earlier connections between mother, 
sea and child. 
The forest is a paradoxical symbol in the stories that feature that 
setting or employ a variation on that image of a world that is not 
within human control. The rose bushes proliferating in ‘The Lady 
of the House of Love’ represent the idea of the fairy-tale forest 
just as much as the setting of ‘The Werewolf’, ‘The Company 
of Wolves’ or ‘The Snow Child’. The fairy-tale forest is physically 
unwelcoming and alluring at the same time. This is most obvious in 
‘The Erl-King’, where the narrator enjoys the ‘delicious loneliness’ 
(p. 97) at the same time as she is aware of the fact that ‘there is no 
clue to guide you through in perfect safety’ (p. 96). 
There is something almost pagan about Carter’s reiteration of 
themes and images of the forest as a place of discovery, sacrifice 
and redemption. Her complex imagery - where trees of the forest 
become the strands of a ‘cat’s cradle’ (p. 97), intricately weaving nets 
or traps, or the grass and the undergrowth grow over the track 
leaving a ‘subtle labyrinth’ (p. 96) that makes it almost impossible to 
follow such good advice as ‘do not leave the path’ (‘The Werewolf’, 
p. 127) - seems to be advancing an agenda for a simpler life, less 
cluttered with the imperatives of acquisition and exchange, and 
more honest self-knowledge for men and women about their place 
in nature.

Structure 
As a collection of stories, the structure of The Bloody Chamber 
is not entirely cohesive. There is no obvious thread or theme that 
flows from story to story in a defined manner, except, perhaps, that 
they are all inspired by and derived from fairy tales. ‘The Bloody 
Chamber’, ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ and ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ 
form a trio of tales about women marrying monstrous men. 
‘Puss-in-Boots’ provides an entertaining comic interlude whose 
exuberant and anarchic spirit gives way to the slower pace of the 
dark and disorienting exploration of the fairy-tale forest in ‘The 
Erl-King’. ‘The Snow Child’ snaps along briskly, its simple 
structure following the fairy-tale formula that events happen in 
reciprocally related patterns of three. ‘The Lady of the House of 
Love’ is an elaborate Gothic romance. The last three stories, ‘The 
Werewolf’, ‘The Company of Wolves’ and ‘Wolf-Alice’, are 
concerned with how to deal with the big, bad wolf. All the stories, 
even ‘Puss-in-Boots’, deal with death and with monsters of the 
imagination. But the ‘bloody chamber’ (p. 35) of the opening story 
provides the title that unites the collection. Considering them as 
tales from ‘the kingdom of the unimaginable’ (‘The Bloody 
Chamber’, p. 36) or as stories of ‘the fated sisterhood’ (p. 27), 
Carter has set up a display of horrors to arouse the emotions and 
celebrate curiosity. 
Narrative techniques 
Carter employs a different narrative style for each story and very 
often within each particular tale moves from one narrative style to 
another. The most complex and subtle experiments with narrative 
form come in ‘The Erl-King’, and the European tradition that is its 
source is likely to be the least familiar of all the stories that form the 
partial foundations of the collection. 
Carter uses first person narrative forms in ‘The Bloody Chamber’, 
‘The Tiger’s Bride’, ‘Puss-in-Boots’ and ‘The Erl-King’. Only in 
the last is the identity of the narrator at first concealed, and the 
position of the narrator to the story is unclear, shifting in and out of 
92 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 95 ===
Critical approaches Narrative techniques 
the action in a dreamlike manner which is intrusive and unsettling. 
Although clearly a female (a girl), the narrator never becomes a 
defined character as the other invented and identifiable - but 
unnamed - narrators do. Puss-in-Boots has an enormous persona 
but is known by a descriptive label which is not really a name and 
lacks individuality, even for a cat. 
‘The Erl-King’ begins its narrative in the third person in the past 
tense; moves to direct address in the second person, present tense; 
and shifts to the third person, future tense, over the first three 
paragraphs. Then it modulates through a couple of changes into 
a first person past tense; this uncertainty of tense and person in 
the narrative voice continues throughout the story. The effect is 
dislocating and disorienting. Time and space are not fixed or definite 
entities. The story proceeds through these uncertainly connected 
moments because Carter retains enough passing detail in the terrain 
described to form a recognisable chronological recount and suggest 
the possibility that a narrative path will emerge. The habit of 
reading, the processing of words that follow words on the page in 
some sort of order, creates an expectation of narrative: something is 
bound to happen. The forest is as much a passing of time as it is a 
place, and the autumn is felt rather than observed as ‘the approach 
of winter’: ‘cold ... grips hold of your belly and squeezes it tight’ 
(p. 96). Carter suggests a place that is not yet dead but is dying: 
‘All will fall still, all lapse’ (p. 97). The vague sense of narrative 
confusion is deliberately and carefully controlled by Carter and it 
creates an eerie sensation of being lost in her forest. The reader 
relies on the narrator to guide us back to the path, but has the 
uneasy suspicion that Carter is not in a hurry to find the quickest 
way home. 
‘Wolf-Alice’ has some touches that suggest the narrator is possibly 
implicated in the action of the story, but these function as a device 
to implicate the reader in the rejection of the wild child more than 
they identify the narrator as other than Angela Carter. The 
opening sentence invites a sense of collusion by the use of the first 
person plural pronoun to draw attention to the child as an alien 
being who does not speak ‘like we do’ (p. 140). Later in the story,

the narrator admits: ‘we secluded her in animal privacy out of fear 
of her imperfection because it showed us what we might have 
been’ (p. 144). 
The narrative voice in the other tales is the third person omniscient 
narrator. It is safe to assume that this is the voice of Carter herself. 
She is not a participant in the events of the stories except in so far as 
she is ordering and creating them. However, she frequently makes 
an aside to the reader in the knowing way that raconteurs will often 
do to change the pace of an anecdote. Occasionally characters will 
speak through the narrator without their speech being formally 
identified as dialogue with conventional punctuation. This often 
happens with the language refrains of fairy tales, such as ‘What big 
eyes you have’ (p. 137) in ‘The Company of Wolves’, and other 
recognisable elements of familiar fairy-tale codes and responses. 
This is a device which implies the words are the inner thoughts of 
the character being made available to us by the narrator in character, 
something that happens naturally when stories are verbally 
exchanged and read aloud. They are allusions that assume a shared 
understanding of the storytelling conventions that signal narrative 
events in fairy tales. For example, in ‘The Lady of the House of 
Love’, the narrative links between an image of the Countess being 
cleaned up after devouring her prey and the introduction of the 
hero through the refrain from ‘Jack and the Beanstalk’: ‘Fee fie fo 
fum / I smell the blood of an Englishman’ (p. 111). The quatrain is 
completed to bring him into the story fully with his arrival at the 
village, under threat of being devoured: ‘Be he alive or be he dead / 
I’ll grind his bones to make my bread’ (p. 112). 
In relation to the idea of metamorphosis as a significant feature 
of the collection of tales as a whole, it is worth considering how 
the narrative changes its form throughout Carter’s tales. Carter 
employs metamorphosis for different effects within the stories, 
changing the forms of the tales as she does so. This metamorphosis 
of narrative form has been the focus of recent critical interest in 
Carter. This is perhaps because forms of sexual identity have 
become less rigid and prescriptive in society, or because the 
feminist critique seems less relevant to the modern world. 
94 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 97 ===
Critical approaches Language and style 
Language and style 
Helen Simpson in her introduction to the 2006 Vintage edition of 
The Bloody Chamber defines Carter as ‘an abstract thinker with an 
intensely visual imagination’ (p. x). This is the chief characteristic of 
Carter’s language use in all the stories in the collection, despite their 
stylistic differences. Whether she is exploring the pseudo-female 
narration of de Sade in ‘The Bloody Chamber’, parodying male 
pomposity in ‘Puss-in-Boots’, or Gothic melodrama in ‘The Lady 
of the House of Love’, Carter seeks to entertain. Her use of 
symbolic objects as ‘a system of signification’ (quoted in Simpson’s 
2006 introduction, p. xv) is conscious for Carter but optional for 
the reader. 
Carter’s visual imagination is complemented by other compelling 
features of her language use. Her vocabulary, ranging from the 
complex and detailed language of classic literature to monosyllabic 
words of sexual reference that still retain their shock value in print, 
is richly diverse and often startling. In her stories, words such as 
‘prothalamion’ (‘The Company of Wolves’, p. 138) and ‘chthonic’ 
(‘The Bloody Chamber’, p. 35) evoke the mythology of earlier 
civilisations and display Carter’s acquaintance with literature of 
academic interest. 
Carter’s portrayal of location and setting, through densely textured 
description of detail and careful consideration of colour, combines 
visual awareness with the other senses. In ‘The Bloody Chamber’ 
the ‘misty blue’ (p. 8) turrets of the Marquis’s castle are presented 
with ‘seabirds mewing’ and the ‘green and purple, evanescent 
departures of the ocean’ (p. 8) to establish the main location for the 
tale. Her use of double negatives - ‘No room, no corridor that did 
not rustle with the sound of the sea’ (p. 9) - emphasises the way 
the landscape seems to melt into the interior while hinting that 
no part of the castle offers any kind of peace or tranquillity. This 
complements the description of the ‘refracted light from the waves’ 
on the walls to create the ‘luminous, murmurous castle’ (p. 9) as a 
place that inspires a sense of awe in the narrator: it is, literally, an 
awful place.

THE BOOK 
Jack Zipes's 
Breaking the Magic 
Spell: Radical 
Theories of Folk

The Bloody Chamber 95 

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===
Language and style Critical approaches 
Carter leaves spaces in the narratives for meaning to be supplied by 
the reader. Sometimes this is done simply through omitting details 
or not stating the obvious fact. In ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ Carter shapes 
an entire paragraph of incomplete sentences to describe the impact 
of the first glimpse of The Beast in his animal form. The paragraph - 
from ‘A great, feline, tawny shape’ to ‘eyes, like twin suns’ (p. 71) - 
functions as a partial list of the imposing features of The Beast and 
subtly conveys a sense of wonder. The narrative seems unable to 
complete a thought or encompass the majesty of the naked beast 
in coherent language. Carter deprives this narrator, otherwise so 
particularly adept with language, of coherent thought under the 
spell of the animal. 
QUESTION 
How far would you 
argue that Carter's 
writing style fits 
with her 
materialist 
principles? 
In other places the grammatical construction of Carter’s prose is 
convoluted and her meaning weaves through elaborately sustained 
multiple clauses. ‘The Lady of the House of Love’ makes extensive 
use of this kind of complex sentence construction. From the first 
sentence, the story tumbles out through a torrent of words 
describing the tangible ‘presences’ of the ‘revenants’ who haunt the 
village in ‘shadows’, ‘sound’ and ‘a sense of unease’ (p. 107). The 
‘beautiful queen of the vampires’ is introduced in one sweeping 
sentence that describes her ‘antique bridal gown’; her ‘dark, high 
house’; the ‘baleful posthumous existence’ of her ‘atrocious 
ancestors’ (p. 107); her sad and somewhat obsessive reading of 
Tarot cards; her desire to escape; and her fatal youth. The narrative 
continues to make use of this feature, as if the narrator simply 
cannot stop herself from embellishing the tale even as it is being 
told. This tale also makes use of the shortened sentence, found in 
the other stories, to create moments of tension or heightened impact 
of details in the story. If anything, the fluidity of the narrative is 
more abruptly shown in ‘The Lady of the House of Love’ by 
sentences such as ‘I will be very gentle’ (p. 119) and ‘Now it is dark’ 
(p. 120), because their brevity is in such stark and ominous contrast 
to the majority of the text. 
Elsewhere in her tales, Carter’s narrative prose approaches a more 
poetic voice, deliberately slipping between two registers of language 
that operate in different ways to create meaning. In ‘The Erl-King’ a 
passage describing the Erl-King’s eyes is punctuated by a line of text 
that stands alone: ‘Eyes green as apples. Green as dead sea fruit’ 
96 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
Critical approaches Language and style 
(p. 103). Carter uses this technique of placing isolated similes to 
punctuate the rhythm of the narrative in other tales. In ‘The Bloody 
Chamber’ Carter morbidly links the disconnected telephone line 
with the murders the Marquis has already committed, again in a line 
that stands alone as if poetically isolated: ‘Dead as his wives’ (p. 29). 
In ‘The Company of Wolves’ Carter makes an alliterative line 
create the emphasis required in converting rational fear of real 
danger into irrational superstition of imaginary terror: ‘Fear and 
flee the wolf; for, worst of all, the wolf may be more than he seems’ 
(p. 130). Again, this line stands alone in the text as a turning point 
in the narrative. 
One of the reasons Carter is appreciated so much by readers with 
a developed literary background is that they feel empowered, 
or perhaps flattered, by her use of allusions to a broad range of 
literature that they recognise. Of course, to operate at many levels 
of meaning is the sign of a skilful writer. By embedding such eclectic 
and often esoteric cultural knowledge into the stories, Carter 
deliberately courts literary recognition. It can be argued that this is 
the least attractive feature of her writing, not only because her self- 
conscious attempts at virtuosity are often unsuccessful, but also 
because - and this is richly ironic for an iconoclastic writer who 
wanted popular success and a broad audience - her work can seem 
tainted with the snobbery of cultural elitism. However, it is a 
feature that has contributed to her burgeoning posthumous 
popularity as a writer for study in academic institutions; the ‘self- 
conscious’ text is an interesting proposition for postmodern literary 
criticism. 
It is inevitable that the effect on readers of multiple layers of 
intertextuality can be arbitrary, and at times Carter can be self- 
indulgent, particularly in those moments where she is striving, 
and sometimes failing, to convey sensations of desire and rapture. 
In ‘The Erl-King’ a number of hyperbolical passages address the 
‘profane mysteries’ (p. 102) of desire and its effects: the description 
of the Erl-King’s embrace ‘made of water’ (p. 102) is lessened by 
the sentence that ends ‘the stream I have become’ (p. 102). Carter’s 
attempt to evoke sensations of emotional fluidity drowns in prose 
that recalls and mangles the cadences of John Keats’s poem ‘Ode to 
a Nightingale’. Her language paradoxically sometimes fails because

CONTEXT 
Arthur Rackham 
(1867-1939) was 
a successful 
children's book 
illustrator, whose 
works include 
Fairy Tales of the 
Brothers Grimm 
(1900). The fantasy 
worlds he created 
were often sinister, 
full of gnarled

and monsters. 
V___ 
it is inconsistently simplistic. In one sentence Carter, trying to 
encapsulate the contradictory response of consolation and 
devastation felt by the girl at her lover’s touch, follows the vivid 
simile of her heart being ‘naked as a stone on the roaring mattress’ 
with the limp phrase ‘the lovely, moony night’ (p. 102). 
An important aspect of Carter’s style is its entertaining playfulness 
and her willingness to enjoy using the ambiguities of meaning 
that exist in familiar, colloquial or conventional language. In 
‘An Interview with Angela Carter’ by Anna Katsavos, originally 
published in The Review of Contemporary Fiction, 14:3, Fall 1994, 
pp. 11-17, Carter comments on the linguistic amusements she 
finds in her writing: ‘there are a whole lot of verbal games in 
[“The Company of Wolves”] that I really enjoy doing, “the deer 
departed,” [p. 129] for example. People very rarely notice these ... 
sort of private jokes with myself and with whoever notices, and I 
used to enjoy doing that very much’ (p. 15). 
Metaphor 
The improper, or unexpected, application of names or descriptive 
terms to objects in order to create a surprising effect is at the heart 
of Carter’s highly ‘improper’ literary art. Though there are many 
metaphoric images employed by Carter, there are a few common 
metaphors that unite many of these stories. Perhaps the most 
striking are the Gothic elements of the stories that emphasise 
meanings associated with journeys from the exterior to the 
interior, penetration of secret spaces, rites of passage, 
transformations and quests. The metaphor of the forest — so 
central to the imaginative landscape of the fairy tale - as the 
depiction of the unknown or the threat of the wilderness beyond 
human civilisation recurs again and again. 
This idea of a wilderness or inhospitable landscape does not always 
appear as a woodland. In ‘The Bloody Chamber’ the new bride’s 
journey terminates at ‘an unknown, never-to-be visited station’ 
(p. 6). The Beast in ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ is first encountered 
in the garden of a mansion isolated in a ‘snow-filled’ (p. 44) world. 
In ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ ‘Beauty’ travels across a ‘wide, flat dish of 
98 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 101 ===
Critical approaches Language and style 
snow’ (p. 62) to The Beast’s ‘megalomaniac citadel’ amid a ‘dead’ 
world (p. 63). In ‘The Erl-King’ it is almost as if the Erl-King and 
his domain are interchangeable: ‘the wood swallows you up’ (p. 96) 
and the Erl-King is ‘the tender butcher’ waiting to ‘skin the rabbit’ 
(p. 100). In ‘The Lady of the House of Love’ the vampire’s castle 
emerges out of the ‘jungle’ of ‘enormous thickets’ (p. 114) of rose 
bushes. In ‘The Werewolf’ the forest resumes its identity as a place 
of ‘Cold; tempest; wild beasts’ (p. 126), while in ‘The Company of 
Wolves’ the forest is a place of ‘teeming perils’ (p. 130) filled with 
‘assassins’ (p. 129): ‘ghosts, hobgoblins, ogres ... witches’ and ‘the 
wolf’ (p. 130). 
The ruined castle, the iconic image of the Gothic genre, is featured 
in one way or another in all of the stories: as a grand symbol of 
power and wealth, or as a representation of the domestic shelter 
from the challenges of the world or a bulwark against the 
psychological pressures that accompany the child becoming an 
adult. The other dominant metaphor is that of ‘the animal’ in 
opposition to ‘the human’; this metaphor is a particularly unstable 
‘figure of speech’ in Carter’s stories. Carter does not have one 
particular view of the relationship of animal and human in these 
stories, and sometimes her view is positive and sometimes it is 
negative. This is one reason why thematic interconnection across 
the collection of tales as a whole is not a simple matter. For example, 
should each of the various transformations of man or woman into 
beast be understood to signify the same view of human society and 
human nature? 
In approaching these stories and considering their meaning, the 
central metaphors of ‘the journey’, ‘the home’, ‘the wilderness’ 
and ‘the beast’ provide a basis for Carter’s rearrangement of the 
traditional tales. The old, patriarchal society she writes to expose 
and challenge is hostile to change, so these ideas are necessarily 
bound up with conflict and contradiction. Whether it is the 
transition from child to adult through life, from innocence through 
experience to new knowledge and understanding, from powerless 
victim to empowered individual, Carter employs a powerful matrix 
of ideas to deal with sex, class and gender as matters of life and 
death. As a feminist and a socialist writer, in some senses, Carter

saw herself as restoring a material basis to the myths of fairy tale 
that, over time, through male narration, had lost the historical 
connection with the original circumstances that created them 
(see Origins). Fairy tales have a moral and didactic purpose. 
They function as warnings and instruction to children, based 
on the collective experience of people with little power in the 
world, on how best to recognise and survive predictable dangers. 
Carter, a modern-day version of ‘Mother Goose’, makes the 
fairy tale a metaphor for her politicised analysis of social class 
and gender. 
Imagery, allusion and intertextuality 
Several central images are reworked throughout most of the stories 
in the collection. Blood is a key image that is obviously related to 
the Gothic element of the tales. It is related at different points to 
menstruation, sexual maturity, the loss of virginity, wounds, pain, 
death and sexual pleasure. In ‘The Bloody Chamber’ the idea of 
the stain or the mark of shame is transferred to the woman by her 
handling of the key to the chamber and her knowledge of its gory 
contents. In ‘The Snow-Child’ it is the evidence of desire after the 
execution of the passion. In ‘The Lady of the blouse of Love’ 
blood is the vampire’s means of survival and the means of her 
release. 
Flowers provide key images in several of the stories: lilies in 
‘The Bloody Chamber’ signify death and mourning through their 
association with funerals; the rose as a symbol of love and beauty 
- also agony and danger - features in ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’, 
‘The Snow Child’ and ‘The Lady of the Flouse of Love’, to very 
different effects. The white rose brings the Beast and Beauty 
together in ‘The Courtship of Mr Lyon’ and symbolises a link of 
some kind of purity between the two: ‘one white, perfect rose’ 
(p. 47) is robbed from the Beast’s garden by the father for his 
daughter; she returns white roses when away from the Beast and 
knows ‘perfect freedom’ (p. 51); her flowers are later discovered to 
be ‘all dead’ (p. 54). For the Countess in ‘The Snow Child’, the rose 
is a weapon. For the heroic cyclist in ‘The Lady of the House of 
Love’, the rose is a powerful but unrealisable portent of impending 
catastrophe on an unimaginable scale. The rose is a potent symbol in 
loo The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 103 ===
Critical approaches Language and style 
the literature and oral tradition of many cultures. The petals of the 
rose, their perfume and colour, have a particular significance. The 
red rose is associated with blood and passion; it is mostly, though 
not always, identified with the female. The many layered petals of 
the rose, likened by artists to female genitalia, have been interpreted 
as being a symbol for the complexity of the universe. In modern 
times the beauty of the rose, the sweetness of its scent and the 
pricking defensive thorns symbolise the paradoxical nature of 
love: beautiful, intoxicating and painful. 
The caged songbird, a potent symbol of the artificial restraints 
placed on human behaviour and the loss of individual freedom, 
particularly for women, is used in ‘The Lady of the House of Love’ 
and functions as a major plot device, in so far as there is any plot, in 
‘The Erl-King’. 
The wolf - ‘Mister Wolf’ - is examined by Carter as a key symbol 
from the imagery of fairy tales and folklore to be subverted. The 
wolf, whose symbolic meaning is derived from the very real 
dangers posed by the wild animal to human beings in their early 
civilisations, represents the threat of unknown and uncontrollable 
nature. The wolf also represents greed, deceit and insatiable 
appetites. There are fewer references to the bravery and strength of 
the wolf as positive attributes, though the female wolf is often 
portrayed as a generous and protective mother. On a simple level, 
wolf tales suggest that children should be wary of strangers. In a 
Freudian, psychoanalytical interpretation, the wolf in fairy tales 
represents a coded acknowledgement of the dangers of suppressed 
or repressed sexual desire. As the embodiment of specific and 
immediate danger, the wolf is an ever present predator. Carter’s 
various attempts at presenting ‘the wolf’ (or rather presenting the 
different dangers she perceives in modern society in the guise of ‘the 
wolf’ in her stories) lead to contradictions that are not easily 
resolved. In ‘The Werewolf’ the threat is female but defeated; in 
‘Wolf-Alice’ the wolf is humanised and reconciled; in ‘The 
Company of Wolves’ the threat is male and the wolf is dehumanised 
to achieve a reconciliation. The Beast in ‘The Courtship of Mr 
Lyon’ and ‘The Tiger’s Bride’ is a variation on the idea of the 
werewolf. In presenting a variety of metamorphoses between 
woman, beast and man, Carter is exploring what it is to be human 
and questioning whether it is possible to understand the reality of 
human nature.

Bruno Bettelheim 
gives a Freudian 
view in The Uses 
of Enchantment: 
The Meaning and 
Importance of Fairy 
Tales (1976), 
examining the 
instructive function 
of fairy tales from a

Carter’s reliance on allusion is not so straightforwardly 
encapsulated. Helen Simpson notes in the introduction to the 2006 
Vintage edition that annotating The Bloody Chamber in detail 
would be a lengthy task (p. xiv). Sometimes Carter’s references to 
works of literature and art are open and direct; often the allusions 
are entwined in several overlapping references that hint at some 
mocking play with meaning. Other explicit references are totally 
fictitious, as if Carter is daring the reader to challenge her erudite 
knowledge of literature and culture while simultaneously mocking 
her own tendency to be esoteric. 
When Carter gives the Romanian aristocrat in ‘The Bloody 
Chamber’ the name Carmilla, she makes a knowing reference to the 
Gothic novella ‘Carmilla’ published in 1872 by Joseph Sheridan Le 
Fanu (1814-73) that featured a predatory lesbian vampire some 
twenty-five years before Bram Stoker’s Dracula appeared. In a 
similar vein, when Carter refers to ‘Vlad the Impaler who picnicked 
on corpses in the forests of Transylvania’ (p. 109) as the ancestor 
and source of the vampire beauty in ‘The Lady of the House of 
Love’, she is recalling a legend that distorts history for the benefit of 
good storytelling, as this account of Vlad Tepes is derived from 
propaganda and the alleged connection between the fifteenth- 
century historical ruler and Stoker’s villain Count Dracula is 
disputable. 
Carter does not confine her allusions to the so-called higher forms 
of culture, literature and art. Children’s literature, horror movies, 
luxury commodities, recognisable brand names and icons of fashion 
are all called into service to support her storytelling. 
Origins 
Although Carter generally takes one particular fairy or folk tale as 
the basis of each story in this collection, recognisable elements of 
other tales constantly reappear at various moments. Carter almost 
makes a seamless connection between all the stories, as if they could 
all be traced back to one great original story that has been long 
forgotten, yet somehow still needs to be told, in different and 
repeating ways. 
102 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 105 ===
Critical approaches Origins 
The traditions of the fairy tales that form the basis of Carter’s tales 
in The Bloody Chamber can be traced back through the centuries 
in various forms across Europe and beyond in the oral tradition. 
In the sixteenth century writers began to make records of these 
tales, and as print technology advanced so the tales became known 
in particular forms, giving rise to textual evidence of adaptation 
and rewriting at different times. Giovanni Francesco Straparola’s 
The Facetious Nights (published in two parts as Le Piacevoli Notti 
in 1550 and 1553, and also referred to as The Pleasant Nights) 
introduced various folk tales to European literature, and certainly 
influenced later literary versions of the tales. Carter’s chief source 
is the seventeenth-century French writer Charles Perrault 
(1628-1703), whose tales provide the source material ‘Bluebeard’, 
‘Puss-in-Boots’, ‘Sleeping Beauty’ and ‘Little Red Riding Hood’. 
Though Perrault is the most well-known source, his versions are 
pre-dated by other writers, and the earliest known written version 
of ‘Puss-in-Boots’ comes from the sixteenth century. 
Later versions of most of these stories were presented by the 
German academics known as the Brothers Grimm in the nineteenth 
century. The Disney animation features of these tales (recently 
satirised in the Shrek series of computer-aided animated films) are 
yet another form of the tales to be taken into account. Some obscure 
sources are cited for particular stories. While ‘The Snow Child’ is 
linked by Helen Simpson (in her introduction to the 2006 Vintage 
edition of The Bloody Chamber, p. xvi) to an unpublished variant 
of ‘Snow White’ collected by the Brothers Grimm, it bears direct 
resemblance to the story ‘Snegorotchka’ incorporated in the French 
illustrator Edmund Dulac’s Fairy Book (1916), introduced as a 
Russian fairy tale. Yet Carter’s use of elements of the tale is very far 
from the sentiments expressed in ‘Snegorotchka’. This is just one 
example of how difficult it can be to define the origin of tales whose 
existence in the oral tradition pre-dates their first written record by a 
considerable margin. 
Carter freely interweaves references and direct quotations from the 
familiar refrains and phraseology of other well-known children’s 
tales: Lewis Carroll’s Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland (1865) and 
Through the Looking-Glass and What Alice Found There (1871) are 
both drawn upon directly and indirectly in several of her tales; the

giant from ‘Jack the Giant-Killer’ (or ‘Jack and the Beanstalk’ or the 
Grimms’ ‘The Brave Little Tailor’) makes his presence felt in ‘The 
Lady of the House of Love’. But it is quite striking that, while 
Carter freely draws upon the magical element of shape-shifting 
creatures, the only fairy or magical being in the collection that is 
taken directly from fairy tale is the wood spirit depicted in ‘The 
Erl-King’. This tale is derived from Germanic and Scandinavian lore 
concerning evil elf creatures that lead children to their destruction 
in the forest. 
While this legacy of stories for children is Carter’s chosen vehicle 
for her writing, and its heritage can be traced to a certain extent, the 
stories are neither backward-looking nor nostalgic. Neither can the 
collection be adequately summarised as an attempt to reclaim those 
old stories for a modern era. The stories that Carter chooses to tell 
are her stories; stories of a twentieth-century woman writing ‘to 
please herself’. In the 1970s that intention was a revolutionary 
manifesto for change, no matter how idealistic or limited in its 
achievements it may seem from the vantage point of the 
twenty-first century. 
104 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 107 ===
Part Four 
Critical perspectives 
Reading critically 
This section provides a range of critical viewpoints and perspectives 
on The Bloody Chamber and gives a broad overview of key debates, 
interpretations and theories proposed since the collection was 
published. It is important to bear in mind the variety of 
interpretations and responses this text has produced, many of 
them shaped by the critics’ own backgrounds and historical 
contexts. 
No single view of the text should be seen as dominant - it is 
important that you arrive at your own judgements by questioning 
the perspectives described, and by developing your own critical 
insights. Objective analysis is a skill achieved through coupling 
close reading with an informed understanding of the key ideas, 
related texts and background information relevant to the text. These 
elements are all crucial in enabling you to assess the interpretations 
of other readers, and even to view works of criticism as texts in 
themselves. The ability to read critically will serve you well both 
in your study of the text and in any critical writing, presentation 
or further work you undertake. 
Original reception 
Ellen Cronan Rose in ‘Through the Looking Glass: When Women 
Tell Fairy Tales’ (in The Voyage In: Fictions of Female Development, 
edited by Elizabeth Abel, Marianne Hirsch and Elizabeth Langland, 
1983, pp. 207-27) was one of the first critics to give a serious critical 
response to The Bloody Chamber, approving the reversal of 
stereotypical gender roles as ‘a critique of the idea of adult 
womanhood sanctioned by patriarchy and a suggested alternative 
to it’. This approval was not shared by Patricia Duncker in 
‘Re-Imagining the Fairy Tales: Angela Carter’s Bloody Chambers’ 
(in Literature and History, 10:1, 1984, pp. 3-14), and she pulled no 
punches in her attack on Carter: ‘But the infernal trap inherent in

(2001) provides

criticism and theory 
can enhance your

THE NET 
You can read some 
of these obituaries 
online: type 'Angela

a search engine. 
the fairy tale, which fits the form to its purpose, to be the carrier 
of ideology, proves too complex and pervasive to avoid.’ In other 
words, Duncker believed Carter had attempted an impossible and 
ultimately pointless task, in that the form of the fairy tale cannot 
be used without the new version being contaminated by the 
ideological content of the original tale. The moral values of the 
stories are embedded in their structure, and Carter’s adaptations and 
changes cannot, according to Duncker, escape that fact. To suggest 
Carter’s stories are trapped by the content of old tales seems, 
however, to ignore what Carter has achieved with them. Changing 
the form of something involves a change in content and meaning: 
a story told by one person will gain very different meanings if it is 
told by another person. Duncker was not the only critic who would 
have preferred women writers not to re-examine the tales of the 
past, but this scornful attitude would not stop other women writers 
attempting their own versions of fairy tales in the 1970s and 1980s. 
Later criticism 
On Angela Carter’s death in 1992, many obituaries were written in 
praise of her writing. However, Merja Makinen, in ‘Angela Carter’s 
The Bloody Chamber and the Decolonization of Feminine 
Sexuality’ (in Feminist Review, 42, Autumn 1992, pp. 2-15), argued 
against the obituarists who eulogised her as a ‘benign magician’, 
creating a mystic aura around her craft as a writer. This approach, 
Makinen suggested, misrepresented the true power of Carter’s 
writing: ‘Carter’s work has consistently dealt with representations 
of the physical abuse of women in phallocentric cultures, of women 
alienated from themselves within the male gaze, and conversely of 
women who grab their sexuality and fight back, of women troubled 
by and even empowered by their own violence’ (p. 3). Renewed 
critical interest in Carter after her death was marked by two events 
in 1994: an academic conference on her work at the University of 
York; and the publication of Flesh and the Mirror: Essays on the 
Art of Angela Carter, edited by Lorna Sage. Sarah Gamble’s 
Angela Carter: A Literary Life (2005), however, notes the way 
that posthumous fame ironically would have been seen as little 
consolation by Carter herself, who was ambitious for recognition 
in her lifetime. 
106 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 109 ===
Critical perspectives Later criticism 
In 1998 Aidan Day in Angela Carter: The Rational Glass felt the 
need to preface his views with the oxymoronic statement ‘Carter’s 
fiction is a bit extreme’ (p. 1), and he is clearly ill at ease with aspects 
of sexuality represented by Carter in The Bloody Chamber, 
particularly ‘female sexuality’ (p. 151). Day believes this to be 
‘difficult ground ... for a male critic’ and he later disqualifies 
himself from commenting on Carter’s representation of female 
desire on the grounds that ‘As a man, I am not competent to judge 
the psychological veracity or otherwise of this image of positive 
female response to male pornographic attention’ (p. 160). It would 
be fair to argue, however, that competence in criticism has nothing 
to do with gender; and Day fails to see the irony in his evasiveness. 
It is precisely his use of gender as an artificial barrier to 
understanding that Carter seeks to overthrow in her stories. 
The controversy concerning the literary merits of Angela Carter’s 
work is far from over, but her position as an object of academic 
study and debate is now an established fact. Alison Easton points out 
in Angela Carter: Contemporary Critical Essays (2000) that though 
‘there was only a handful of studies before the late 1980s’, the range 
of critical opinion has widened dramatically since her death, despite 
the fact that this is ‘not a long time to establish a field of study’ 
(pp. 5, 6). Dominic Head caustically comments in The Cambridge 
Introduction to Modern British Fiction, 1950—2000 (2002): ‘Angela 
Carter is a significant writer; but she does not deserve the status of 
(by some margin) the most-written-about post-war British novelist. 
If the number of academic theses devoted to an author were to taken 
as a reliable measure of the author’s relative importance, Carter 
would emerge as the single literary giant of the period’ (pp. 3-4). 
Other critics have found Carter’s tendency to return to certain tales 
more intriguing. Jacqueline Pearson in her foreword to Re-Visiting 
Angela Carter: Texts, Contexts, Intertexts, edited by Rebecca 
Munford (2006), emphasises the reiterative nature of her creative 
intertextuality: ‘If for Carter allusion helps to provide a language 
for ambiguity, one way of achieving this is to tell and retell certain 
central narratives’ (p. ix). Munford in ‘Angela Carter and the 
Politics of Intertextuality’, her introduction to this 2006 work, goes 
some way to explaining why the academic study of Carter has 
expanded exponentially: ‘the alluring literariness of Carter’s writing

Later criticism Critical perspectives

the NET 
A comprehensive 
bibliographical 
resource, the 
Feminist Theory

feminism 
is most often at work as part of a feminist analysis of the social 
fictions which shape identity and experience’ (p. 16). Typically, this 
seems to downplay the point that Carter is more interested in 
attacking the concrete social conditions that produce and maintain 
those fictions. Gina Wisker in ‘Behind Locked Doors: Angela 
Carter, Horror and the Influence of Edgar Allan Poe’ (in Re- 
Visiting Angela Carter: Texts, Contexts, Intertexts, edited by 
Munford, 2006) draws attention to the influence of Poe, but points 
out a crucial difference in that ‘where pleasure and pain are 
separated in Poe, in Carter their sexual connection is explicit; sex 
and torture are aligned’ (pp. 189-90). 
Jago Morrison in Contemporary Fiction (2003) summarises The 
Bloody Chamber as ‘an archaeological investigation of gender 
representation, coupled with a set of creative attempts at subversion’, 
thereby assigning it to a neat, but dismissive, pigeonhole in feminist 
fiction (p. 168). Morrison seems to regard Carter’s stories as 
academic exercises in creative writing that have failed. 
Sarah Gamble in Angela Carter: A Literary Life (2005) observes a 
neat psycho-biographical connection in the way that the fairy-tale 
narrative mode ‘places the figure of the story-teller centre stage’ 
(p. 156) and how Carter created a new public identity as a 
storyteller following the publication of The Bloody Chamber. 
Gamble recognises that Carter won this identity on her own terms, 
as ‘pleasing her audience by providing them with a comfortably 
reassuring read did not feature on her list of priorities’ (p. 156). 
Gamble asserts what she believes is evidence of the enduring value 
of Carter’s work: ‘Most of Carter’s writing remains in print: it is 
read by general readers and studied at universities, and has lost 
none of its capacity to shock, startle and delight’ (p. 205). 
Contemporary approaches 
Feminism 
Feminist criticism has many different strands - radical, bourgeois, 
lesbian, heterosexual - each of which view Carter in very different 
ways according to their own political and sexual orientation. 
Patricia Duncker in ‘Re-Imagining the Fairy Tales: Angela Carter’s 
108 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
Critical perspectives Contemporary approaches 
Bloody Chambers’ (in Literature and History, 10:1, 1984, pp. 3-14) 
voices a particularly hostile perspective: ‘Carter envisages women’s 
sensuality simply as a response to male arousal. She has no 
conception of women’s sexuality as autonomous desire.’ Certainly 
Carter won little praise from feminist critics for The Sadeian 
Woman (1979), and the reaction to her attitude towards the Marquis 
de Sade and sexuality in that work was applied to The Bloody 
Chamber. Particular attention was given to Carter’s suggestion in 
The Sadeian Woman that there might be a worthy social role for a 
‘moral pornographer’ (‘a phrase’, she later remarked in an interview 
with Anna Katsavos, ‘that got me into a lot of trouble with ... some 
of the sisters’). Carter writes in The Sadeian Woman, in the first 
chapter ‘Polemical Preface: Pornography in the Service of Women’, 
that a ‘moral pornographer might use pornography as a critique of 
current relations between the sexes’. In her view, such an artist 
‘would not be the enemy of women, perhaps because he might 
begin to penetrate to the heart of the contempt for women that 
distorts our culture’. Carter also thought that the writings of the 
Marquis de Sade suggested the possibility of ‘moral pornography’ 
because she discerned in the libertarian aspects of his work ‘an 
ideology not inimical to women’. This was not a popular view 
among feminists and remains controversial. Andrea Dworkin in 
Pornography: Men Possessing Women (1981) denounces Carter as a 
‘pseudo-feminist’ (p. 84); Susanne Kappeler in The Pornography of 
Representation (1986) accuses Carter of being cut off from reality 
and of attempting a kind of literary rehabilitation of de Sade, ‘the 
multiple rapist and murderer’ (p. 133). Duncker simply dismisses 
Carter’s belief that de Sade ‘put pornography in the service of 
women’ as ‘utter nonsense’ (in ‘Re-Imagining the Fairy Tales: 
Angela Carter’s Bloody Chambers’, p. 8).

Postmodernist

fairy-tale form by making use of the sexual relationships embedded

Sally Keenan’s ‘Angela Carter’s The Sadeian Woman: Feminism

revisions of fairy tales and her ‘scandalous liaison with the book 
on Sade’. Her view of that connection is provocative: ‘If Carter’s 
analysis of Sade’s texts emphasises their fairy-tale-like abstraction, 
then her revision of traditional fairy tales serves to highlight the 
pornographic nature of the stereotypes of women that they have 
re-circulated.’

central position it has hitherto occupied in analyses of this text’

commentaries become more interested in the shifting and 
interrelated forms of the narratives that constitute one whole text 
for the reader. Armitt insists in ‘The Fragile Frames of The Bloody

facing the reader of these ten stories is that they seem always to be 
dissolving into each other’ (p. 97).

Critical perspectives Contemporary approaches 
perspectives and Rosi Braidotti’s concept of ‘nomadic feminism’, 
which he defines as ‘Never at home in orthodoxies or in final 
statements, it maintains a balance between the need to act to change 
the world and the need to think through these actions and the world 
in an open-ended way’ (p. 206). 
Marxism 
Marxism is, above all, the philosophy for social change based on the 
economic analysis of capitalism established by Karl Marx (1818-83) 
and Friedrich Engels (1820-95) in the nineteenth century. Culture 
and ideology have always been seen as arenas of political conflict 
by Marxists, and this tradition of cultural commentary has since 
spawned an entirely academic school of Marxism that confines its 
revolutionary activity to the contemplation of literature using the 
formulas and phraseology of Marxist political analysis. 
From the early part of the twentieth century, different attitudes to 
art, culture and literature have been present in Marxist thought. The 
philosophy of Marxism is defined as dialectical materialism. This 
implies an understanding of the world being in constant movement 
and change through contradictory processes. The philosophical 
term ‘dialectical’ is related to the art of debating propositions 
through contradiction to achieve clear understanding. Marxism 
sees all things as containing their own ‘dialectic’ of contradiction, 
a conflict of opposites, that will govern their future development. 
Eeon Trotsky (1879—1940), a leading revolutionary in Russia in 
1917, viewed literature with dialectical materialist understanding in 
his article ‘The Social Roots and the Social Function of Literature’ 
(1923): ‘Artistic creation is always a complicated turning inside out 
of old forms, under the influence of new stimuli which originate 
outside of art.’ It is not difficult to see a connection between this 
statement and Carter’s achievements in The Bloody Chamber, 
especially the reference to the subversion of old forms. 
When the Communist movement split in the 1920s and Stalinist 
‘socialist realism’ was dogmatically adopted as the new orthodoxy 
by Soviet critics, many so-called Marxists in Western society 
followed the party line. Literature was only considered ‘politically 
correct’ if it depicted reality in certain ways. Anything else was 
routinely denounced as ‘bourgeois reactionary fantasy’. This has

between passion

been a dominant tendency in Marxist literary criticism, though 
not the only one. Consequently, many adherents of the school of 
Marxist literary criticism exemplified by Georg Lukacs have real 
difficulties in dealing with works of fantasy such as The Bloody 
Chamber. Applying a simplistic definition of Marxism to her blend 
of materialism and fantasy does little to help readers understand 
either Carter or Marxism. 
Robert Eaglestone in ‘The Fiction of Angela Carter’ (in 
Contemporary British Fiction, edited by Richard J. Lane, Rod 
Mengham and Philip Tew, 2003) believes that Carter’s work is not 
‘strictly material in a political (say, Marxist sense)’ (p. 204). Carter 
was a materialist (her world outlook being ‘this world is all there is’) 
and would certainly have agreed with Trotsky’s view that ‘However 
fantastic art may be, it cannot have at its disposal any other material 
except that which is given to it by the world of three dimensions 
and by the narrower world of class society’ (‘The Social Roots and 
the Social Function of Literature’, 1923). Trotsky’s 1924 speech 
against political control in the field of imaginative literature insisted 
that Marxists should recognise ‘the fundamental relationship 
between a reader and a work of art. ... Let us take, for instance, 
such an elementary psychological feeling as fear of death. This 
feeling is characteristic not only of man but also of animals. In man 
it first found simple articulate expression, and later also artistic 
expression’ (‘Class and Art - Culture Under the Dictatorship’). 
In approaching The Bloody Chamber from the perspective of 
Marxist literary criticism, the text becomes a prism which refracts 
the social conditions that produced it: the political death of the 
post-war social consensus in Britain (see Historical background). 
Carter voices opposition to what she perceived to be reactionary 
forces in society, from a feminist and socialist perspective. Issues of 
gender dominate the tales. For example, the restricted choices and 
limited economic independence of the female characters, all of 
whom are expected to play out the roles defined for them by 
the male characters; the centrality of female empowerment and 
self-reliance; the necessity of resisting and escaping tradition 
through creative personal choice; and, above all, a recognition that 
changes in a patriarchal society will come from women, not from 
men, as men do not have motives to give up their positions of 
power. But these issues of gender are not easily disentangled 
112 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 115 ===
Critical perspectives Contemporary approaches 
from issues of class for Carter, and class struggle is a key tenet of 
Marxism. As a materialist, Carter grounds her fantasy in carefully 
selected realistic detail; as a storyteller, Carter explores how fantasy 
frees the imagination from the constraint of realism. A Marxist 
approach takes into account Carter’s background as a student of 
medieval literature as an important influence on her style (see 
Literary background: Medieval influences). 
For Carter, the active resolution of the personal and social 
contradictions that her characters cannot avoid is the most 
important aspect of the tales: her characters act to make changes 
happen in their lives; they are not passive victims of circumstance. 
In this respect they can be viewed as icons of the ‘call to action’ 
of the 1970s feminist and socialist milieu. A Marxist approach to 
The Bloody Chamber as ‘a complicated turning inside out of old 
forms’ may be more relevant than some feminist critics might admit.
