Angela Carter’s life and works 
Angela Olive Stalker, born in Eastbourne, Sussex, on 7 May 1940 
to Olive (nee Farthing) and Hugh Stalker, took the surname Carter 
from her first husband and retained it as her professional identity 
after her divorce. Her father was Scottish, a journalist and veteran 
of the First World War; her mother was brought up in a mining 
community in Yorkshire. Angela was evacuated during the Second 
World War to Wath-upon-Dearne, a village in the South Yorkshire 
mining area, and lived with her maternal grandmother for the 
first five years of her life. The family influence of her maternal 
grandfather, dead some time before Angela was born, can be traced 
in her attitudes. He was a socialist, a member of the Independent 
Labour Party, a trade unionist and deeply committed to self- 
education and self-improvement. Her mother was a grammar 
school girl and did not share her grandfather’s outlook, and 
Angela Carter would later describe her relationship with her 
mother as difficult and more complex than her relationship 
with her father. 
Angela returned to Balham, south London, after the end of the 
Second World War. Suffering from anorexia and issues of self¬ 
esteem, she failed to realise the early signs of academic potential she 
demonstrated as a young girl, and when she was eighteen her father 
secured her an apprenticeship position as a junior newspaper 
reporter on the Croydon Advertiser. In 1960 she married Paul 
Carter and moved with him to Bristol. Unable to get work as a 
journalist, she enrolled as a mature student at Bristol University. 
She chose to study medieval literature and began her career as a 
writer while still an undergraduate. Her first novel, Shadow Dance, 
was published in 1966, the year after she graduated. 
Within a few years, Angela Carter was becoming a successful 
author, with several novels published, and she was beginning 
to establish a reputation for herself as an award-winning writer. 
The Magic Toyshop (1967) won her the John Llewellyn Rhys Prize. 
114 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 117 ===
Background Angela Carter’s life and works 
Two novels followed which, along with Shadow Dance, are usually 
referred to as her Bristol trilogy: Several Perceptions (1968, which 
won the Somerset Maugham Award in 1969) and Love (1971). 
These novels are united by being set in the 1960s Bristol ‘scene’ and 
by the features of the Gothic genre they subvert, particularly the 
idea of women as victims of men. Carter disliked these novels being 
categorised as ‘Gothic’, as she believed she was writing in a realistic 
manner about the Bristol she knew. In 1969 she separated from her 
husband and left Europe, via America, for Japan. When she 
returned to England in 1972, she had no publisher and had been 
absent from the literary scene for three years. The momentum of 
her early career seemed to be faltering. In the five years before 
The Bloody Chamber appeared she had two novels published - 
The Infernal Desire Machines of Doctor Hoffman (1972) and The 
Passion of New Eve (1977) - together with a collection of short 
stories, Fireworks: Nine Profane Pieces (1974). 
The publication of The Bloody Chamber in 1979, her second 
volume of short stories (shortly preceded by her equally 
controversial non-fiction work The Sadeian Woman), was a factor 
in the restoration of her career. The controversy surrounding the 
work, establishing her as an ‘extreme’ writer, not only attracted 
the feminist critics and media reviewers, but brought her to the 
attention of a wider general readership. Carter would go on to write 
two other volumes of short stories, one of which, American Ghosts 
and Old World Wonders (1993), was published after her death. In 
1995 all Carter’s short stories, including her four collections, were 
gathered together into one volume with an introduction by Salman 
Rushdie: Burning Your Boats: Collected Short Stories (see Further 
reading for a list of Carter’s works, including poetry, drama and her 
non-fiction writing). 
By the mid 1980s, Angela Carter was a well-established author and 
journalist, an acknowledged presence in radio drama, and beginning 
to make her mark in the film industry with the adaptation of her 
written work for the cinema and television. She had a new family - a 
second husband, Mark Pearce; and a son, Alexander (born in 1983) 
- and she was developing her role as a teacher in various higher 
education establishments in the UK, America and Australia.

CONTEXT 
'Then arose new 
architects who after 
the manner of their 
barbarous nations 
erected buildings in 
that style which we 
call Gothic': so said 
the Florentine artist 
Giorgio Vasari 
(1511-74), regarded 
as the first art 
historian. Gothic 
architecture 
features high 
vaulted ceilings, 
pointed arches and 
buttressed walls. 
Most examples in 
Britain are large 
church buildings 
such as the

V_ 
In 1984 one of her most acclaimed novels, Nights at the Circus, 
was published, and the film The Company of Wolves, directed by 
Neil Jordan, was released. Nights at the Circus is a magic realism 
extravaganza, starring a trapeze artist called Fewers, who was 
hatched from an egg and grew real wings at puberty. Carter’s last 
novel, Wise Children, a feast of carnival magic and comic realism, 
was published only a few months before her death from lung cancer 
on 16 February 1992. It is true that an untimely death can spark 
renewed interest in an artist or writer. This was certainly the case 
with Angela Carter but, unusually, that burgeoning posthumous 
interest has not faltered, nor has her reputation as ‘a bit extreme’ 
(as described by Aidan Day in Angela Carter: The Rational Glass, 
1998, p. 1) been affected by the years that have passed since her 
death. It is ironic but perhaps not unsurprising that, as a woman 
and a writer who spent her life ‘talking back’ to authority, her work 
was only truly embraced by the literary critics and academics when 
she was no longer around to answer them. 
Literary background 
The Gothic tradition 
The term Gothic originally described a particular style of art in 
medieval Europe. Typically surviving in its most obvious form in 
architecture, Gothic style dominated much of the culture at that 
time. The Goths were a pagan tribe in northern Germany who 
took control of parts of the Roman Empire as it collapsed. The 
Gothic style is associated with crude, grotesque and exaggerated 
representation and, to some extent, is a fusion of early Christian 
and pagan symbolism. Whereas the Christian ideal would seek to 
inspire a sense of a divine presence through awe and mystery, the 
pagan influence represents the divine as concrete aspects of nature. 
The style came to be seen as a primitive attempt to incorporate the 
power of the wilderness within a civilising structure, and was 
viewed, during the Renaissance and the age of Enlightenment, 
as the sign of a superstitious and brutal epoch. (The medieval 
influence on Carter is discussed in more detail below: see 
Medieval influences.) 
116 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 119 ===
Background Literary background 
Works of literature that have been defined or labelled as Gothic 
share certain features: notably a preoccupation with extremes 
of sensation, perception and location; and an emphasis on the 
supernatural. These characteristics have been recycled in more or 
less conscious fashion by the writers included in or flirting with this 
genre over the last three hundred years or so. The Gothic sensibility 
in literature was a reactionary attempt to warn, in a sensational and 
entertaining way, about the social and individual consequences 
of adopting unconventional ideas or going against the accepted 
beliefs of the time. The thrills are provided by the transgressive 
sense of going beyond the boundaries of acceptable behaviour. 
The challenge to the normative conduct of society and individuals 
is usually accompanied by dire consequences for the protagonist. 
But the genre is not limited to questions of moral or ethical 
imprudence. 
Carter positively revels in extreme sensations, perceptions and 
locations. The gruesome display of dead wives in ‘The Bloody 
Chamber’, the disorientation of the senses in ‘The Erl-King’, and 
the descriptions of wilderness and the palazzo (p. 63) in ‘The Tiger’s 
Bride’, for example, demonstrate how Carter exaggerates the Gothic 
content of fairy tales. Only ‘The Bloody Chamber’ and ‘Puss-in- 
Boocs’ do not explicitly take the supernatural as the central 
characteristic of the tale. ‘Puss-in-Boots’ seems less obviously 
Gothic in its lecherous comedy, but Carter certainly delights in 
the bawdy and murderous adultery her anarchic heroes undertake. 
The narrator in ‘The Bloody Chamber’ experiences the thrills and 
near-fatal consequences of transgression. In The Bloody Chamber 
Carter is both sensational and entertaining while promoting 
unconventional ideas that challenged the accepted beliefs of 
her time. The collection proposes some radical alternatives to 
the traditional fairy tale or Gothic ‘consequences’ of personal 
action. 
Early Gothic 
It is possible to trace a cultural connection between medieval 
mystery plays, one of the means by which Christian doctrines were 
communicated to an illiterate society, and later works of drama that

CONTEXT 
Frankenstein was 
written by Mary 
Shelley in response 
to a literary 
challenge from 
Lord Byron in 
1816. The same 
competition to 
produce a horrific 
tale also caused 
John Polidori,

'The Vampyre', the

to be published in

employ allegorical or supernatural devices. Shakespeare’s plays 
Macbeth (1606) and Hamlet (1601) are notable precursors of the 
Gothic sensibility, with their ghostly yet unreliable exhortations 
to action and their preoccupation with insanity. Feigned madness, 
the passions of revenge and elaborate crimes of murder are features 
of John Webster’s revenge tragedy The White Devil (1612), also 
regarded as a text that foreshadowed the Gothic literature of the 
eighteenth century. 
Eighteenth- and nineteenth-century Gothic 
Horace Walpole’s The Castle of Otranto (1764), Ann Radcliffe’s 
The Mysteries of Udolpho (1794) and Matthew G. Lewis’s The 
Monk (1796) are considered to be the ‘classic’ Gothic tales that 
established the conventions of the genre in English literature. The 
characters tend to be rather two-dimensional, suffering excesses of 
emotional stress through fear and terror, mostly related to 
supernatural phenomena, and the events take place in unfamiliar 
locations distanced in time from the present moment. 
Jane Austen’s Northanger Abbey (begun in 1798 and published 
posthumously in 1818) shifted the focus of genre from the perceived 
hysteria of the victim as caused by external intimidation to the 
psychological interior world of her characters. Some commentators 
see this as a response to the waning credibility of the Gothic 
convention, though the popularity of horrific and sensational stories 
seems undiminished in the burgeoning mass culture. Mary Shelley’s 
Trankenstein, or The Modern Prometheus, which appeared in 1818, 
relies so little on the traditionally identified elements of Gothic 
fiction that it almost appears to be outside the genre altogether. 
However, it is an enquiry into the nature of resurrection, and all 
the essential questions of life and death are posed by the existence 
endured by Frankenstein’s creature. As such, the elements of 
supernatural power, fearful locations and terrible madness are 
presented to support Shelley’s monstrous investigation of what 
it is to be human. Emily Bronte’s Wuthering Heights (originally 
published under the gender-disguising name Ellis Bell in 1847), with 
its intricate narrative structure and desolate setting, also features a 
mysterious and violent man who destroys a strong woman, amid 
ghosts, passions and revenge. 
118 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
Background Literary background 
Later Gothic 
The modernity of the later Victorian Gothic novels explores 
the darker aspects of the human psyche in the familiar setting 
of the modern world. This is a conscious attempt to question, 
by bringing the terrors of the past into conflict with the assumed 
certainties of the present, the direction of social progress. Robert 
Louis Stevenson’s The Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde 
(1886) and Oscar Wilde’s The Picture of Dorian Gray (1890) use 
the topical notion of multiple personality to explore the dual 
nature of humanity as man-beast, god-devil, angel-demon. Angela 
Carter, in The Bloody Chamber, reworks this typical theme of the 
Gothic genre, not only in the magical transformations throughout 
the collection, but also in the way these transformations explore 
the dual nature of humanity as both human and animal. As in 
H. G. Wells’s The Island of Doctor Moreau (1896), science and 
religion are called into question as the authors dwell on the 
potential horrors and glories within the individual. Bram Stoker’s 
Dracula (1897) imports European folklore in a highly theatrical, 
eroticised and melodramatic combination of Gothic elements that 
portrays civilisation threatened by an ancient menace. In this way 
these novels reflect the powerful revolutionary changes taking 
place in the nineteenth century that inspired and still influence 
our understanding of personal identity and social responsibility. 
In the twentieth century the influence of modernism and the 
all too real horrors of human history saw the genre in decline. 
Its influence has fragmented into particular and identifiable 
commercial markets such as science fiction and horror, though it 
has always remained a certain presence in the mainstream. Critics 
have applied the term Southern Gothic to the works of Tennessee 
Williams (1911-83) and other American writers, in a bid to place 
them as a recognisable subgenre derived from European literature. 
Aspects of the Gothic were used by writers such as Daphne du 
Maurier (1907-89) and Mervyn Peake (1911-68); and other 
writers, such as Anne Rice (b.l 941) and the master of modern 
horror, Stephen King (b.l947), incorporate elements of the Gothic 
genre in their work as they continue to explore the dark horrors of 
the human imagination.

THE BOOK

Medieval influences 
Angela Carter’s wide interests in literature and philosophy are 
represented in the depth and range of intertextual reference 
incorporated in her stories. She is a self-consciously and defiantly 
literary writer. Her interests in medieval literature are present in 
The Bloody Chamber in two important and definitive ways. 
Medieval creators of text were conscious that they were writing 
for an audience that would hear their tales rather than read them. 
The element of the human voice in the narrative is something 
Carter consciously strives to achieve. She is always present as the 
storyteller, even when the narrative voice is nominally given to a 
character within the story. Visual imagery before the era of literacy 
had a different social significance; for four hundred years or so, 
society has been dominated by the medium of print. Carter is a 
highly visual writer, and her use of symbol, metaphor and allegory 
is a return to that preliterate age in a literary form. 
Allegory is a significant narrative device in medieval literature and 
works in a variety of ways to convey meaning to the reader on 
different levels. Medieval texts were written to be read aloud to 
non-readers, and allegory is another way of showing to the reader 
the meanings within the story. Just as Everyman stands as a 
character in a drama in medieval morality plays, and as a device 
providing a point of reference and identification for every person 
in the audience, so too are Carter’s characters more than merely 
themselves to the reader, particularly the female reader. The reader 
is shown the moral journey of these characters and invited to 
identify with their personal dilemmas. 
Postmodern and Mannerist influences 
The influence of the developing postmodernist perspective is also 
present in Carter’s writing (see also Contemporary approaches), 
but it can be seen as a kind of formalised attraction to the idea of 
‘systems of significance’ as embodied in the work of early 
twentieth-century theorists such as Ferdinand de Saussure 
(1857-1913) and Charles Sanders Peirce (1839-1914) and their 
120 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
Background Historical background 
European followers Roland Barthes (1915-80) and Jean Baudrillard 
(1929-2007), who elaborated the theory of semiotics. But Carter 
distanced herself from the reactionary relativism of postmodernism 
which conflicted with her materialist perspective. She considered 
the postmodern perspective to be self-referential and introverted, 
an academic exercise that involved merely writing ‘books about 
books’ without any connection to the world beyond literature; 
she compared this approach to the sixteenth-century Mannerist 
movement in art. This comparison was not meant as a compliment, 
but to show how she understood postmodernism to be the opposite 
of a progressive development in theory. 
But there are also connections between Carter’s work and the idea 
of Mannerism as an artistic perspective, particularly its rejection of 
notions of symmetry and harmonious equilibrium. Mannerist 
painters are typically regarded as having depicted emotional 
intensity through the use of distorted perspectives, intricate 
composition and vivid imagery. They also pursued ambiguity in a 
way that is also present in Carter’s work. Carter was careful in her 
construction of her stories and concerned that her readers found her 
meaning, but was also quite relaxed about being read in different 
ways, and at different levels of meaning, by different readers: her 
stories acknowledge and allow that possibility. 
Historical background 
The post-war period in which Angela Carter grew up was 
dominated by the cold war between the former allies against 
fascism. The USSR emerged from the Second World War as a major 
international power and Eastern Europe disappeared behind what 
Winston Churchill defined in 1946 as the ‘Iron Curtain’. Britain was 
no longer able to sustain its former imperial position and America, 
as the dominant capitalist power in the West, began to have greater 
influence on British culture. 
Post-war Britain was dominated by the ‘make do and mend’ 
approach. Rationing for some items did not stop until the mid 1950s 
and Britain was, as it had been during the war, heavily dependent 
on American aid. The introduction of the welfare state in 1945,

Historical background Background 
CONTEXT 
William Beveridge 
(1879-1963) was a 
Liberal economist 
commissioned by 
the British 
government to 
report on the ways 
Britain could be 
rebuilt after the 
Second World War. 
He recommended 
that the 
government 
should take steps 
to overcome the 
five 'Giant Evils' 
facing society - 
'Want, Disease, 
Ignorance, Squalor 
and Idleness' - and 
in 1945 the Labour 
government 
announced the 
introduction of the 
welfare state as 
suggested in his 
1942 Beveridge 
Report. 
v_/ 
©CHECK 
THE NET 
Search the Internet 
for more 
information about 
the cold war and 
other major 
historical and 
political incidents of 
the 1970s and 1980s. 
with free health care and education for all, was a significant social 
reform that shaped British life in the latter part of the twentieth 
century. This was followed in 1948 by the radical and innovative 
establishment of a National Health Service with medical treatment 
available to all. 
The austerity of the immediate post-war period in Britain gave 
way to Harold Wilson’s era of the ‘white heat of technology’ that 
optimistically looked forward to a future where advances in 
science would solve many of humanity’s material problems. The 
introduction of the oral contraceptive pill in 1960 was to have a 
profound impact on the social relations between men and women. 
The so-called ‘baby boom’ generation, born in the aftermath of 
the war, came of age during the 1960s and defined that decade as 
one of protest against the mistakes of previous generations. The 
assassination of the American president John F. Kennedy on 
22 November 1963 is often viewed as a turning point in the cold 
war, while the America war in Vietnam became the focus of 
unprecedented levels of international protest. Old values and 
traditional perspectives were challenged as the televisual age 
shrank the world to fit into a smaller screen. 
The 1970s brought political scandals in America; economic upheaval 
with the price of oil rapidly rising; increasing unemployment and 
industrial strikes in Britain; challenges to ‘male chauvinism’ by the 
feminist movement; and the turn towards monetarism in Britain 
replacing the mixed-economy approach that attempted to harness 
market forces for public benefit. 
Margaret Thatcher’s policy of privatisation in the 1980s was 
opposed by the trade union movement, and legislation was 
introduced to limit trade union power for the benefit of free 
market entrepreneurs. Britain went to war against the Argentinian 
occupation of the Falkland Islands in 1982, and after the war the 
Tories were returned to power with a huge majority. The political 
confrontations that characterised the mid 1980s, most of all the 
miners’ strike of 1984, redefined British society. Thatcher’s 
‘Victorian values’ predominated and her view that ‘There is no such 
122 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
Background Historical background 
thing as society. There are individual men and women and there 
are families’, given in a interview with Woman's Own magazine 
(31 October 1987), summed up that change in outlook completely. 
The end of the cold war era was in sight when Mikhail Gorbachev, 
president of the USSR, announced the policies of glasnost and 
perestroika as a rejection of Stalinist dictatorship in 1985. Within six 
years the USSR had ceased to exist and the cold war was officially 
over. South Africa ended years of apartheid rule and released 
Nelson Mandela after twenty-seven years in prison. With the 
demolition of the Berlin Wall starting in 1989, the ‘Iron Curtain’ in 
Eastern Europe was no more. But in China in the same year, student 
protests for reform were crushed in Tiananmen Square. In the 1990s 
satellite communications made the Internet available to the public, 
creating the possibility of instant personal interaction between 
individuals around the world. America, as the dominant superpower 
in the world, took its main European ally, Britain, into the first war 
in the Gulf in 1991. 
Carter was very much aware of herself as a late twentieth-century 
woman. As she once stated: ‘The sense of limitless freedom that I, as 
a woman, sometimes feel is that of a new kind of being. Because I 
simply could not have existed, as I am, in any other preceding time 
or place. I am the pure product of an advanced, industrialised, post¬ 
imperialist country in decline’ (‘Notes from the Front Line’, in On 
Gender and Writing, edited by Michelene Wandor, 1983, p. 73).

=== PDF PAGE 140 ===
Further reading 
Background reading continued 
Betty Friedan, The Feminine Mystique, Penguin, 1992 
Originally published in 1963, this work provided inspiration for the second-wave feminists 
Emily Gerard, The Land Beyond the Forest: Facts, Figures, and Fancies from Transylvania, 
Harper & Brothers, 1888 
You can read an excerpt of an essay by Gerard which was later incorporated into her 1888 work 
online: type the author’s name and ‘Transylvanian Superstitions’ into a search engine 
Germaine Greer, The Female Eunuch, Granada edition, 1981 
This influential feminist text was first published in 1970; in it Greer writes: ‘The first significant 
discovery we shall make as we racket along our female road to freedom is that men are not free, 
and they will seek to make this an argument why nobody should be free’ 
Jacob and Wilhelm Grimm, Complete Fairy Tales, Routledge edition, 2002 
Contains every one of the two hundred and ten tales collected by the Grimms 
Dominic Head, The Cambridge Introduction to Modern British Fiction, 1950-2000, 
Cambridge University Press, 2002 
A thorough study of post-war British fiction which places novels in their historical and social 
context; includes a chapter on gender and social identity 
Richard Jacobs, A Beginner's Guide to Critical Reading: Readings for Students, 
Routledge, 2001 
Includes essays on Emily Bronte’s Wuthering Heights and John Milton’s Paradise Lost, Jacobs relates 
the latter text to the political and historical context of the English Civil War, and examines Milton’s 
work as ‘anti-authoritarian’ in relation to his role as a ‘radical pamphleteer’ 
Susanne Kappeler, The Pornography of Representation, Polity Press, 1986 
Attacks Carter’s treatment of pornography in her 1979 work The Sadeian Woman 
Anna Katsavos, ‘An Interview with Angela Carter’, The Review of Contemporary Fiction, 
14:3, fall 1994, pp. 11-17. 
Katsavos’s interview with Carter took place in 1998 
Zachary Leader (ed.), On Modern British Fiction, Oxford University Press, 2002 
Seventeen essays examining post-war British fiction by renowned writers such as Ian McEwan, 
Martin Amis and Elizabeth Jane Howard 
Howard Phillips Lovecraft, Supernatural Horror in Literature, Dover Publications, 1973 
Brief critical history of supernatural horror in literature 
138 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
Further reading 
Niall Lucy, Postmodern Literary Theory: An Introduction, Blackwell, 1997 
Caricatures the ‘liberal humanist’ view that ‘literature is produced by writers of genius’ and opposes 
it with the view that ‘could be seen as leading to the development of postmodern literary theory’: 
‘writers themselves are produced (to varying degrees) by forces beyond their control’. Lucy believes 
this to be ‘revolutionary’ because it ‘denies that a literary text is the expression of an author’s 
intentions, feelings and linguistic mastery’ (p. 1) 
Lucien Malson, Wolf Children and the Problem of Human Nature, Monthly Review 
Press, 1972 
Psychiatrist Malson examines more than fifty cases 
Karl Marx, Capital, Volume 1, translated by Ernest Mandel and Ben Fowkes, Penguin, 1976 
This opening volume of Marx’s Das Kapital was first published in 1867 
Karl Marx, Economic and Philosophical Manuscripts of 1844, Lawrence & Wishart, 1973 
Unpublished during Marx’s lifetime 
Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels, The Communist Manifesto, edited by David McLellan, 
Oxford University Press, 1992 
First published in 1848; this edition includes the subsequent prefaces written by Marx and Engels 
Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels, The German Ideology, Lawrence & Wishart, 1970 
Written in 1845-6, this text was not published in full until 1932 
Kate Millett, Sexual Politics, Doubleday, 1970 
A key text on the development of modern feminism 
Laura Mulvey (ed.), Visual and Other Pleasures, Macmillan, 1989 
Contains Mulvey’s essay ‘Visual Pleasure and Narrative Cinema’, originally published in Screen, 
16:3, Autumn 1975, pp. 6-18 
John O’Brien, Harlequin Britain: Pantomime and Entertainment, 1690-1760, John Hopkins 
University Press, 2004 
Examines the emergence of eighteenth-century British pantomime and its effect on British culture 
Ferdinand de Saussure, Course in General Linguistics, translated by Roy Harris, 
Duckworth, 1983 
The basis of twentieth-century linguistics and much modern literary criticism, this work was 
originally published in 1916

Contains a chapter on forests in the Western imagination

An accessible and fascinating guide to English beliefs and traditions

Privately printed for members of the Society of Bibliophiles, the original Le Piacevoli Notti was

Carter’s ‘Notes from the Front Line’ can be found in this work

Examines the lasting power of fairy tales and looks at the continuing links between literature and 
folklore. First published in 1979, the 2002 edition is revised and extended 
Jack Zipes (ed.), The Great Fairy Tale Tradition: From Straparola and Basile to the Brothers 
Grimm, W. W. Norton & Co., 2001 
Argues that the Brothers Grimm took the majority of their tales from literary sources, rather than 
collecting them from oral traditions

Literary terms 
allegory a story or a situation with two different meanings, where the straightforward 
meaning on the surface is used to symbolise a deeper meaning underneath. This secondary 
meaning is often a spiritual or moral one whose values are represented by specific figures, 
characters or events in the narrative 
alliteration the repetition of the same letter or sound in a stretch of language, most often 
at the beginnings of words or on stressed syllables 
allusion a passing reference in a work of literature to something outside the text; may 
include other works of literature, myth, historical facts or biographical detail 
ambiguity the capacity of words and sentences to have double, multiple or uncertain meanings 
analogy (analogue) the comparison between an idea or object for the purpose of 
clarification or explanation; the illustration of an idea by means of a similar one; a literary 
parallel 
anthropomorphism the attribution of human characteristics and qualities to non-human 
objects or abstractions; also known as prosopopoeia (see also personification) 
aside when a character speaks in such a way that some or all of the other characters on stage 
cannot hear what is being said; or they address the audience directly. It is a device used to 
reveal a character’s private thoughts, emotions and intentions 
cliche a widely used expression which, through overuse, has lost impact and originality 
colloquial everyday speech used by people in informal situations 
commedia dell’arte a type of comic drama evolved in sixteenth-century Italy using standard, 
recognisable plots usually based on love intrigues. Plot and dialogue were often improvised, 
and the performance would often include mimic, farce, slapstick buffoonery, dancing and 
music 
conceit an extended or elaborate concept that forges an unexpected connection between 
two apparently dissimilar things 
couplet a pair of rhymed lines of any metre 
denouement the point in the story where the whole plot has finally unfolded (from the 
French for ‘untying a knot’) 
The Bloody Chamber 141 

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===
Literary teams 
deus ex machina in Greek drama a god was sometimes lowered onto the stage by a piece 
of machinery in order to assist the hero or untangle the plot (from the Latin for ‘god out 
of a machine’). The phrase is often used today to indicate an unexpected and frequently 
improbable twist in the plot 
dialect a manner of speaking or form of language peculiar to an individual or particular 
region or class; it differs from the standard language of a country 
didactic a didactic text is one that expresses its author’s intention to teach or instruct the 
reader in an explicit or implicit manner 
Enlightenment a term used to describe a literary and philosophical movement in Europe 
that had a profound faith in the powers of human reason. Beginning in the late seventeenth 
century, it continued for much of the eighteenth century 
l 
epigraph a quotation or comment placed at the beginning of a piece of work, relevant to the 
theme or content 
eponymous adjective describing the character who gives his or her name to the title of a play 
or novel 
euphemism an inoffensive word or phrase substituted for one considered offensive or hurtful 
farce a form of drama, though it can apply to any literary form, in which the comic is taken 
to extreme lengths of absurdity 
feminism a social perspective advocating the rights and claims of women arising in 
nineteenth-century Europe and re-emerging in the so-called ‘second wave’ of feminist 
writers who gained success in the 1970s. Although not a unified perspective, it had particular 
influence in the field of literary criticism in the latter part of the twentieth century 
Gothic a term originally applied to a medieval architectural style considered to be barbaric 
and uncivilised, in reference to the Dark Age tribe of the Goths. Later this term was taken 
into the identification of culture, literature or art that makes use of such stylistic reference 
points; though as a literary genre the term implies more than mere reference to architectural 
description - Gothic literature deals with passion, mystery, supernatural or horror, and 
usually employs a medieval setting such as a haunted castle or abbey 
142 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===
Literary terms 
hyperbole deliberate exaggeration, used for effect (from the Greek for ‘throwing too far’) 
ideology shared beliefs of a culture which are taken for granted and thus never questioned 
imagery descriptive language which uses images to make actions, objects and characters 
more vivid in the reader’s mind. Metaphors and similes are examples of imagery 
intertextuality the explicit or implicit referencing of other texts within a work of literature. 
It is designed to put the work in the context of other literary works and traditions and 
implies parallels between them 
irony the humorous or sarcastic use of words to imply the opposite of what they normally 
mean; incongruity between what might be expected and what actually happens; the ill-timed 
arrival of an event that had been hoped for 
magic realism fiction or other art forms such as painting or cinema which combine realism 
with fantastic elements. It reminds the reader or observer that all art is created and invented. 
By the 1980s it was an established form of fiction; authors regarded as magic realists include 
Gabriel Garcia Marquez (b.l928), Salman Rushdie (b.1947), Emma Tennant (b.1938) and 
Angela Carter 
Mannerism a term that refers to European art and architecture of c. 1520-c. 1600, between 
the Italian High Renaissance and the baroque. Mannerism is identified by its insistent use 
of artificial qualities: distorted forms, exaggerated postures and unnatural lighting effects; 
stylistically it is a rejection of the ideals of harmonious composition found in Leonardo da 
Vinci (1452-1519), Raphael (1483-1520) and Michelangelo (1475-1564) 
Marxism the political and economic theories of Karl Marx (1818-83) and Friedrich Engels 
(1820-95). In Marxism the class struggle is the basic force behind historical change. The 
economic conditions of a period determine or profoundly influence the political, social and 
cultural ideology. Marxist literary criticism is concerned with the relationship between the 
historical conditions and the ideology expressed in literature and which produces the work 
materialism in philosophy the idea that nothing exists except the material world and its 
shifts or changes, as opposed, for example, to religious or spiritual belief (Marxism is a 
materialist theory) 
melodrama a form of drama in which extravagant and sensational deeds and thinly drawn 
characters occur, with strong elements of violence, sexuality and evil and a simplistic moral 
or judicial code 
The Bloody Chamber 143 

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===
Literary terms 
metaphor a figure of speech in which a word or phrase is applied to an object, a character or 
an action which does not literally belong to it, in order to imply a resemblance and create an 
unusual or striking image in the reader’s mind 
metre the rhythmic arrangement of syllables in poetic verse 
modernism a movement that spanned the period from the closing years of the nineteenth 
century to the start of the Second World War; the term covers all the creative arts. While 
many artists and writers of different outlooks and achievements are categorised as 
‘modernist’, certain principles are common, for example the rejection of established rules, 
traditions and conventions, and the exploration of abstract forms of expression. Authors 
such as T. S. Eliot (1888-1965), James Joyce (1882-1941), Virginia Woolf (1882-1941), 
D. H. Lawrence (1885-1930) and Samuel Beckett (1906-89) have all been called modernists 
narrative a story, tale or any recital of events, and the manner in which it is told. First 
person narratives (‘I’) are told from the character’s perspective and usually require the reader 
to judge carefully what is being said; second person narratives (‘you’) suggest the reader is 
part of the story; in third person narratives (‘he, ‘she’, ‘they’) the narrator may be intrusive 
(continually commenting on the story), impersonal or omniscient. More than one style of 
narrative may be used in a text 
narrator the voice telling the story or relating a sequence of events 
omniscient narrator a narrator who uses the third person narrative and has a godlike 
knowledge of events and of the thoughts and feelings of the characters 
oxymoron a figure of speech in which words with contradictory meanings are brought 
together for effect 
paradox a seemingly absurd or self-contradictory statement that is or may be true 
parody an imitation of a work of literature or a literary style designed to ridicule the original 
pastiche a form of imitation which uses pieces of the work of another writer either for comic 
effect or for acknowledgement of quality 
pathetic fallacy the attribution of human feelings to objects in nature and, commonly, 
weather systems, so that the mood of the narrator or the characters can be discerned from 
the behaviour of the surrounding environment 
144 The Bloody Chamber 

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===
Literary terms 
patriarchy a social system of government in which power is held by the elder males and 
passed to the younger males exclusively; although the term pre-dates feminism, the concept 
of patriarchy is central to the feminist perspective 
personification the treatment or description of an object or an idea as human, with human 
attributes and feelings 
postmodernism a vague term, often controversial, used to refer to tendencies and 
developments in literature and other arts since the mid twentieth century. Characterised by a 
distrust of ideologies and theories, postmodernist literature is often non-traditional, featuring 
parody, pastiche, fatalism, irony and an eclectic approach. Forms of criticism embraced by 
postmodernism include feminism, Marxism and psychoanalytic criticism. Richard Dawkins 
comments on postmodernism: ‘Never once have I heard anything that even remotely 
approaches a usable, or even faintly coherent, definition’ {A Devil’s Chaplain: Reflections 
on Hope, Lies, Science, and Love, 2003, p. 7) 
protagonist the principal character in a work of literature 
psychoanalytic criticism in literature, applying an approach to understanding a text by 
analysing the unconscious motivations of, for example, the characters 
realism literary portrayal of the ‘real’ world, in both physical and psychological detail, 
rather than an imaginary or ideal one. Victorian novels sometimes described themselves 
in this way 
revisionism in literature the rewriting or retelling of a well-known text or story in 
which characters and/or plot are changed in order to challenge the view presented in the 
original. These stories cover myths, folk and fairy tales, religious stories and narratives that 
have become embedded in the culture 
rhetoric structured and patterned language used in formal speech-making 
satire a type of literature in which folly, evil or topical issues are held up to scorn through 
ridicule, irony or exaggeration 
semiotics Swiss linguist Ferdinand de Saussure (1857-1913) proposed that language is made 
up of a system of signs. A sign is something that stands for something else (for example the 
word ‘chair’ stands for the ‘thing that we sit on’). The word ‘chair’ is the signifier and ‘thing 
that we sit on’ is the signified. His ideas led to the development of semiotics, which examines 
signs in such fields as literary theory, psychology and anthropology 
The Bloody Chamber 145 

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
Literary terms 
simile a figure of speech which compares two things using the words ‘like’ or ‘as’ 
symbol something that represents something else by association 
symbolic, symbolism investing material objects with abstract powers and meanings greater 
than their own; allowing a complex idea to be represented by a single object 
synonym a word that means the same or nearly the same as another word 
tragedy in its original sense, a drama dealing with elevated actions and emotions and 
characters of high social standing in which a terrible outcome becomes inevitable as a result 
of an unstoppable sequence of events and a fatal flaw in the personality of the protagonist. 
More recently, tragedy has come to include courses of events happening to ordinary 
individuals that are inevitable because of social and cultural conditions or natural disasters
